Likewise, clenching both hands into fists, holding the left fist as though poised to strike, and bringing the right fist close to the chest while threatening with the index finger is the mudrā of Abhimukha. Clenching both hands into fists as if whirling them, stretching out the legs, and raising the fists upward as if about to strike is the mudrā of Durdharṣa.

Likewise, joining both hands with hollowed palms and bending the two little fingers and the two thumbs inward is the mudrā of the cudgel of Vaiśravaṇa. From that very mudrā, forming the two index fingers into the loop of a noose is the noose, the mudrā of the son of Vāsuki. Likewise, joining both hands with hollowed palms and embracing all the fingers with the two index fingers is the mudrā of the wish-fulfilling jewel of Kṣitigarbha. From this very mudrā, joining the two index fingers in the shape of a sword is the mudrā of the sword of Ākāśagarbha. From the sword mudrā, bending the two ring fingers inward is the mudrā of Sarvanīvaraṇaviṣkambhin. From that sword mudrā, bending the two thumbs inward is the mudrā of the Victor Śāntimati.

Likewise, holding both hands with the palms turned up and raising the two ring fingers is the mudrā of the flower. From this very mudrā, bringing the two middle fingers to the palms is the mudrā of scented ointment. From that very mudrā, bringing the two thumbs to the two middle fingers is the mudrā of incense. From this very mudrā, embracing the two thumbs with the two little fingers is the mudrā of the water offering. Extending the two thumbs is the mudrā of the lamp. Hiding the thumbs and drawing in the index fingers is the mudrā of giving the torma.

Likewise, joining both hands together, joining the thumbs and index fingers together, and extending the two ring fingers is the mudrā of Vajra Sarasvatī. From the mudrā of Vajra Sarasvatī, bending the two index fingers inward and turning the two little fingers toward the thumbs is the mudrā of vajra laughter. From the mudrā of vajra laughter, raising the two little fingers is the mudrā of the vajra eyebrow. From the mudrā of the vajra eyebrow, bending the two index fingers in the shape of eyes and setting them against the sides of the middle fingers is the mudrā of the vajra eye. From the mudrā of the vajra eye, extending the two middle fingers is the mudrā of the vajra ear. From the mudrā of the vajra ear, bending the left middle finger is the mudrā of the vajra navel. From the mudrā of the vajra navel, bending the right middle finger and extending the left is the mudrā of the vajra cheek. From the mudrā of the vajra cheek, extending the two index fingers, bending their tips and joining them is the mudrā of the vajra portico. From the mudrā of the vajra portico, raising the left thumb and bending the right is the mudrā of the vajra waist. From the mudrā of the vajra waist, separating the two ring fingers and spreading them outward is the mudrā of the vajra sign. From the mudrā of the vajra sign, bending the two ring fingers just slightly and pointing them downward is the mudrā of the vajra thigh. From the mudrā of the vajra thigh, extending the left ring finger just slightly is the mudrā of the vajra calf. From the mudrā of the vajra calf, extending the right ring finger and bending the left inward is the mudrā of the vajra foot.

Crossing both hands and binding them in the manner of the vajra bond, and holding the two index fingers in the threatening gesture, is the mudrā of the vajra hand. From the mudrā of the vajra hand, bending the left index finger is the mudrā of all the male servants. From this very mudrā, bending the right index finger is the mudrā of all the female servants. From that very mudrā, opening out and extending the right middle finger is the mudrā of all the male messengers. From this very mudrā, opening out and extending the left middle finger is the mudrā of all the female messengers. Forming both hands into the vajra bond as before and raising the left thumb is the mudrā of all the male doorkeepers. From this very mudrā, extending the right thumb is the mudrā of all the female doorkeepers.

Likewise, opening both hands, joining the palms as though they meet, and placing them on the crown of the head is the mudrā of the Youth Born from the Crown. Likewise, joining both hands with hollowed palms and extending the right thumb is the mudrā of all the vajra gods. From this very mudrā, drawing in the right thumb and extending the left is the mudrā of vajra song. As before, joining the hands with hollowed palms and bending the right index finger is the mudrā of the vajra yakṣa. From the mudrā of the vajra yakṣa, bending the two middle fingers and extending the two index fingers is the mudrā of all the vajra yakṣiṇīs. From that very mudrā, extending the two middle fingers is the mudrā of the vajra gandharva. From the mudrā of the vajra gandharva, bending the right ring finger is the mudrā of the vajra asura. From that very mudrā, extending the right ring finger is the mudrā of the vajra garuḍa. From this very mudrā, bending both little fingers is the mudrā of the vajra kinnara. From that very mudrā, bending the left index finger is the mudrā of the vajra mahoraga. From that very mudrā, extending it is the mudrā of vajra fire. From that very mudrā, bending the two middle fingers is the mudrā of the vajra rākṣasa.

From that very mudrā, extending them is the mudrā of the vajra rākṣasī. From this very mudrā, bending the two ring fingers is the mudrā of the vajra ḍākinī. From that very mudrā, extending them is the mudrā of the vajra subduer. From that very mudrā, bending the two little fingers is the mudrā of the vajra shadow-subduer. Extending them is the mudrā of the vajra apasmāra.

Joining both hands back to back in the manner of hollowed palms, raising all the fingers, and setting the two thumbs against their respective palms is the mudrā of the Great Fierce Vajra. From that very mudrā, placing the right thumb in its own position is the mudrā of the vajra that stops birth. Likewise, placing the left thumb in its natural position is the mudrā of the vajra that stops aging. Likewise, raising the two thumbs is the mudrā of the vajra that stops sickness. From that very mudrā, placing the two thumbs beside the two index fingers is the mudrā of the vajra that stops poverty. From this very mudrā, opening the two index fingers and joining them with the two thumbs is the mudrā of the vajra that stops decline. From that very mudrā, embracing the left index finger with the right thumb, and likewise embracing the right index finger with the left thumb, is the mudrā of the vajra that stops death.

Joining both hands with hollowed palms, raising the two middle fingers side by side, embracing the ring and little fingers with the index fingers, and placing the two thumbs beside them is the mudrā of the uṣṇīṣa of the perfect buddhas. Likewise, joining both hands with hollowed palms, bending the two ring fingers and the two little fingers inward, setting the middle fingers side by side, bending the two index fingers and joining them at the sides, and placing the two thumbs beside them is the mudrā of the uṣṇīṣa of all the buddhas. From this very uṣṇīṣa mudrā, extending the two index fingers is the mudrā of the vajra of supreme knowledge. From this very mudrā, raising the two ring fingers is the mudrā of Śumbha, the King of Vidyās. From that very mudrā, bending the two index fingers and holding them so that they do not touch the middle fingers by about the width of a barleycorn is the mudrā of Susiddhikara.

Likewise, joining both hands together and spreading all the fingers in the manner of a bell is the lotus mudrā of Avalokiteśvara. Forming both hands like an opened lotus, as though meeting, is the mudrā of Pāṇḍaravāsinī, the White-Robed One. Forming that same mudrā as though slightly opened is the mudrā of Mahāsthāmaprāpta. As before, joining both hands together, bending the middle, ring and little fingers inward, setting the two index fingers side by side, and joining the two thumbs beside them is the mudrā of noble Tārā. Likewise, joining both hands back to back, embracing each other, the fingers interlocked and bound in the manner of a chain, is the mudrā of the vajra chain.

The Empowerment of Noble Vajrapāṇi, the ninth section.

Then all of them, the gods, nāgas, yakṣas, gandharvas, asuras, garuḍas, kinnaras, mahoragas and the rest, bowed to the bodhisattva Vajrapāṇi and said, "Blessed One, for the sake of the bodhisattvas who practice the secret mantra door of bodhisattva conduct and strive to accomplish the siddhis, we too shall offer in this great maṇḍala our own mudrās, which serve as signs. Through them, those sons and daughters of good family will swiftly attain the siddhis."

When they had said this, the bodhisattva, the great being Vajrapāṇi, said to that entire assembly, "Sons of good family, you have set out for the benefit of many beings. Excellent, excellent! Therefore, sons of good family, declare your own mudrās in full."

Then Brahmā, lord of the Sahā world, declared his own mudrā: "One makes the right hand into a fist, extends three fingers, and joins the index finger and thumb together. Blessed One, this is my mudrā of Brahmā's club."

Then Śakra (Indra), lord of the gods, declared his own mudrā: "One joins both hands with hollowed palms, puts the ring and little fingers inside, raises the two middle fingers side by side, and places the two index fingers and thumbs beside them. This is my vajra mudrā, which accomplishes the aims of all rites."

Then the Four Great Kings declared their own mudrās: "One clenches both hands into fists and raises the two thumbs; this is the mudrā of the cudgel of the great Vaiśravaṇa. From this very mudrā, placing the two index fingers above the two thumbs is the mudrā of the club of Virūḍhaka. From the club mudrā, forming the two little fingers in the manner of a noose is the noose mudrā of Dhṛtarāṣṭra. From the noose mudrā, bending the two thumbs inward and raising the two index fingers side by side is the vajra mudrā of Virūpākṣa."

Then Pāñcika said this: "Clenching both hands into fists and raising the two middle fingers side by side is the mudrā of the spear of Pāñcika. From this very mudrā, raising the two thumbs and the two little fingers as well, in the manner of a spear, is the mudrā of the trident of Āṭavaka. Clenching the fists as before and raising the two thumbs is the mudrā of the hammer of the Eight-Armed One. Likewise, joining both hands with hollowed palms, joining the two middle fingers back to back, bending the two index fingers and joining them to the third joint of those same fingers, joining the two thumbs to the sides of these two, and placing the remaining fingers side by side is the mudrā of Jambhala Varuṇa. From this very mudrā, bending the two thumbs inward is the mudrā of all the yakṣas. Raising those same thumbs is the mudrā of all the yakṣiṇīs. From that very mudrā, placing the two index fingers above the thumbs is the mudrā of all the gods. From that very mudrā, opening the two middle fingers and joining them on the inner side is the mudrā of all the gandharvas. Bending those same fingers inward is the mudrā of all the vidyādharas. Raising them, separating them in the manner of tongues of flame, and bending the two little fingers inward is the mudrā of all the asuras.

From this very mudrā, separating the two ring fingers slightly as well and keeping the rest as before is the mudrā of all the rākṣasas. Covering the face with the left hand and licking the palm with the tongue is the mudrā of all the ḍākinīs. Likewise, joining both hands together, interlacing all the fingers and raising them is the mudrā of all the asuras. Turning both hands palm upward and making them into a curved shape is the mudrā of all the nāgas.

Likewise, joining both hands back to back with the two thumbs embracing each other is the mudrā of the discus of Viṣṇu. Clenching both hands into fists and raising the two middle fingers side by side is the mudrā of the spear of Śaṃkara. From the spear mudrā, putting the two index fingers and the two ring fingers inside and raising the rest is the mudrā of the brandished trident of the Averter of Austerities. Clenching the fists as before, setting the middle fingers side by side and bending them at the third joint is the mudrā of the short spear of Kārttikeya, son of the Kṛttikās. Clenching the right hand into a fist and raising the thumb in the ordinary way is the mudrā of the club of Yama. Likewise, clenching the fist and extending the index finger is the mudrā of the short spear of Raudrī, the consort of Rudra. Forming that same hand as though holding a lotus and placing the index finger at the tip of the thumb is the mudrā of the lotus of Brāhmī, the consort of Brahmā. Touching the thumb to the little finger and forming the remaining three fingers like a banner is the mudrā of the vajra of Aindrī, the consort of Indra. Turning that same hand downward in the manner of a bell is the mudrā of the bell of Kālarātrī, the Black Night. Likewise, clenching the fist and raising the index finger is the mudrā of the crest of the Lord of Death. Clenching that same hand into a fist and raising the thumb slightly, curved, is the mudrā of Vārāhī, the consort of the Boar. Raising that same hand forcefully is the mudrā of the skull of Cāmuṇḍī.

Likewise, clenching the right hand into a fist and setting the ring finger and thumb side by side is the mudrā of the shadow-subduing grahas. From that very mudrā, setting the thumbs side by side is the mudrā of the female shadow-subduers. From this very mudrā, joining the little finger as well is the mudrā of the apasmāra graha. Joining the index finger there as well is the mudrā of the subduing grahas. From that very mudrā, drawing out the little finger is the mudrā of the graha of sorcery. "Clenching the left hand into a fist and raising the thumb in the ordinary way is the mudrā of the cudgel of Tumburu," so the Victors have taught.

Likewise, clenching the fist, putting the thumb inside and extending the index finger is the mudrā of the spear of Jayā. Likewise, clenching the fist and raising the middle finger is the mudrā of the sword of Vijayā. Likewise, clenching the fist and raising the ring finger is the vajra mudrā of Ajitā. Likewise, clenching the fist and raising the little finger is the mudrā of the wheel of Aparājitā.

Likewise, joining both hands with hollowed palms, extending all the fingers like feathers, raising the two thumbs in the middle and holding them unmoving is the mudrā of the wings of the garuḍa Vinata. From this very mudrā, raising the right index finger is the mudrā of the son of Vāsuki, king of the nāgas. Likewise, the mudrās of all the nāgas should be applied in sequence as is fitting; these are the mudrās of all the nāgas.

Turning the faces of both hands toward oneself and, in the manner of lightning flashing out, touching the fingertips together and scattering them in the four directions is the mudrā of all lightning. Likewise, joining both hands together and holding them in the manner of a garland is the mudrā of all the clouds of the rainy season. Likewise, joining both hands with hollowed palms and touching the tips of the two middle fingers is the mudrā of the noose of Varuṇa. From that very mudrā, putting the two thumbs inside is the mudrā of the Moon. From this very mudrā, raising the two middle fingers is the mudrā of the Sun. Putting the two little fingers inside is the mudrā of Sarasvatī. From this very mudrā, putting the two ring fingers inside and raising the two little fingers is the mudrā of all the great rivers. Likewise, joining both hands with hollowed palms and then separating them, holding the left hand as though holding a water flask and placing the right in the gesture of granting fearlessness, is the mudrā of Agni. Showing the right hand while first uttering oṃ is the mudrā of all the sages. Raising both hands and bending the index and little fingers just slightly is the mudrā of all the mountains. Likewise, joining both hands, crossing the two thumbs and crossing the two index fingers as well is the mudrā of the great encircling mountain range.

Seated on a yogic seat, one who is wise places both hands at the level of the navel as though holding an alms bowl; this is the mudrā of the alms bowl. Likewise, raising the right hand in the manner of granting fearlessness is the mudrā of granting fearlessness. Likewise, turning the right hand palm upward and holding it in the manner of granting the supreme is the mudrā of granting the supreme. Clenching the right hand into a fist, extending the index finger and holding it at the forehead with a wrathful frown: this mudrā is the mudrā that terrifies Māra. Likewise, clenching the right hand into a fist, raising the middle and ring fingers and placing the thumb beside them is the mudrā of the eye.

Likewise, joining both hands, interlacing them, clenching the fingers into fists, raising the two index fingers, bending them and joining them in the shape of a circle: this is the mudrā of the noose. Likewise, joining both hands, clenching them into fists, raising the index finger, bending its first joint, and holding the rest crossed is the mudrā of the hook. From that hook mudrā, bending the two middle fingers and holding them against it is the mudrā of the tathāgatagarbha. From that very mudrā, extending the ring finger and keeping the rest as they are is the mudrā of the navel of the tathāgatas. From that very mudrā, extending the little finger and keeping the rest as they are is the mudrā of the waist. Likewise, joining both hands with hollowed palms, bending the two index fingers and putting them inside, bending the two ring fingers, the two little fingers and the two thumbs just slightly, and setting the two middle fingers side by side: this is the mudrā of the sheath.

From this very mudrā, raising the two ring fingers upward is the mudrā of sealing the great vajra boundary. From that very mudrā, bending the two middle fingers and extending the thumbs is the mudrā of the great protection. Likewise, extending the two index fingers and putting the two thumbs inside is the mudrā of the vajra blazing all around. Likewise, clenching both hands into fists, extending all the fingers and embracing them from the sides with the two index fingers is the mudrā of the vajra armor. From that very mudrā, joining the two ring fingers and the two thumbs is the mudrā of the vajra tongue. From that very mudrā, bending the two index fingers and the two ring fingers and joining them inside, raising the two thumbs and bending them just slightly, holding the middle fingers turned toward the sides, and keeping the two ring fingers as before is the mudrā of the vajra mouth. From that very mudrā, putting the two index fingers inside, bending them and raising them is the mudrā of the vajra fangs. From that very mudrā, spreading the two index fingers and bending the first joint is the mudrā of the vajra of the discriminating knowledges.

Likewise, joining both hands in the manner of hollowed palms and putting the two thumbs and the two little fingers inside, joined together, is the mudrā of the vajra that holds the ten powers. From that very mudrā, bending the tips of the two thumbs and joining them is the mudrā of the vajra of the applications of mindfulness. From that very mudrā, placing the two ring fingers above the two thumbs is the mudrā of the vajra that realizes all wisdom. From that very mudrā, bending them and placing them beside the two index fingers is the mudrā of loving-kindness. Likewise, joining both hands with hollowed palms, embracing the two ring fingers with the two middle fingers, placing the two index fingers above the two thumbs, and keeping the rest as before is the mudrā of the vajra of granting the supreme.

Clenching the left hand into a fist, bending the index finger and bringing the ring finger close to it is the mudrā of the vajra hook of all the female doorkeepers. From that very mudrā, bending all the fingers just slightly and raising the middle finger is the mudrā of the utpala vajra. Likewise, clenching the right hand into a fist, raising the index and middle fingers and making them slightly curved is the mudrā of the single-edged vajra sword with a hilt.

Likewise, joining both hands with hollowed palms and bending the first joint of the two index fingers is the mudrā of Gaganāmala, Stainless as the Sky. From this very mudrā, crossing the two thumbs is the mudrā of Born from the Feet of the Tathāgata. Putting those same thumbs inside is the mudrā of the Wearer of Variegated Robes. Likewise, putting the two index fingers inside is the mudrā of Entering and Abiding in the Equality of All Phenomena. From this very mudrā, holding the two middle fingers crossed is the mudrā of Abiding Free of Elaboration. Likewise, joining both hands and joining the two thumbs and the two little fingers is the mudrā of Born from the Armor of the Tathāgata. From this very mudrā, raising the middle and ring fingers is the mudrā of the Great Proclaimer. Likewise, joining both hands, interlacing the fingers, forming them like the moon and binding them, and setting the two thumbs against the sides is the mudrā of Gaganāmala, Stainless as the Sky. From this very mudrā, drawing out the two little fingers and touching their tips to the two thumbs is the mudrā of Emerging from the Ten Powers. From this very mudrā, drawing out the two ring fingers and keeping the two little fingers as before is the mudrā of Overcoming Infinite Space.

From the mudrā of the vajra of granting the supreme, placing the two thumbs in the palms is the mudrā of Delightful to Behold. Drawing out the thumbs and bringing them close to the index fingers is the mudrā of Cāritramati. From this very mudrā, joining the middle fingers and the thumbs is the jewel mudrā of Sthiramati. From this very mudrā, extending the two index fingers is the mudrā of Ratnākara. From this very mudrā, touching the tips of the thumbs and ring fingers and extending the middle fingers is the mudrā of Ratnamudrāhasta. From this very mudrā, joining the thumbs and little fingers and bending the index fingers is the mudrā of Ever Abiding in Diligence.

Likewise, joining both hands, putting the two ring fingers inside, pressing the tips of the two middle fingers with the two index fingers and joining the two thumbs at the sides is the mudrā of the wheel of the Blessed One, the great king, the Cakravartin. From this very mudrā, putting the two thumbs inside is the mudrā of Great Laughter. From this very mudrā, extending the index fingers is the mudrā of the great blazing light rays of this same Blessed One. Embracing the two middle fingers with the two index fingers in the manner of armor is the mudrā of the armor of the Blessed One. Bending the right index finger is the mudrā of the ears of this same Blessed One. Bending the right index finger and extending the left is the mudrā of his eyebrows. Bending both index fingers is the mudrā of his nose. Likewise, embracing the two middle fingers and the two ring fingers with the two index fingers in the manner of armor, bending them just slightly and bringing the two thumbs close to them is the mudrā of the uṣṇīṣa of the Blessed One, the great king, the Cakravartin. Turning that same mudrā downward and placing it between the eyebrows is the mudrā of the ūrṇā hair of the Blessed One. Likewise, extending the index fingers, raising the thumbs and bending them is the mudrā of the mouth of the Blessed One. From this very mudrā, putting the two thumbs inside and raising them again is the mudrā of the fangs. From that very mudrā, putting the two ring fingers inside and raising them outward is the mudrā of the tongue. From this very mudrā, joining the two index fingers at the sides in the manner of a ring is the mudrā of the cheeks. From this very mudrā, putting the two index fingers inside and joining them beside the two middle fingers is the mudrā of the kosko. From the uṣṇīṣa mudrā, raising the two little fingers and placing them at the heart is the mudrā of the śrīvatsa. Likewise, joining both hands in the manner of hollowed palms and joining the two index fingers in the manner of rings against the sides of the two middle fingers is the mudrā of the eyes of the Blessed One. From this very mudrā, putting the two middle fingers inside is the mudrā of the navel.

Putting the two ring fingers inside as well is the great mudrā of the speech of the Blessed One. Likewise, joining both hands with hollowed palms, bending both index fingers inside the two little fingers and holding them in the shape of a vajra is the mudrā of Born from the Vajra of the Blessed One. From that very mudrā, keeping the two little fingers as before and placing the two index fingers at the sides in the shape of a vajra is the mudrā of Born from the Vajra Limbs. From this very mudrā of Born from the Vajra Limbs, bringing the two thumbs close to the two middle fingers and keeping the rest as before is the mudrā of Hard as a Vajra.

Likewise, crossing both hands, turning the left downward and the right upward, touching the tips of the thumbs and the two little fingers, and holding the remaining fingers in the shape of a trident is the mudrā of the vajra of both. From this very mudrā, binding the little fingers and thumbs in the manner of the vajra bond is the mudrā of the vajra bracelet, which is the mudrā of sealing the boundary. From this very mudrā, drawing in both the right and left index fingers is the mudrā of the vajra that binds the ground. From this very mudrā, extending the index fingers as before and bending the middle fingers of both the right and left hands is the mudrā of the vajra garland. From the mudrā of the vajra garland, extending the middle fingers and bending the right ring finger is the mudrā of the vajra that holds the whole body. From that mudrā of the vajra that holds the whole body, extending the ring finger and bending the first joint of the index finger is the mudrā of the vajra that destroys the sky. Likewise, placing the index finger of the left hand also in the palm in the manner of a peg is the mudrā of the kīla vajra. From the mudrā of the kīla vajra, drawing in all the fingers is the mudrā of vajra auspiciousness. From the mudrā of vajra auspiciousness, raising all the fingers and holding them so is the mudrā of the vajra canopy. From that very mudrā, holding the fingers apart is the mudrā of the vajra trident. From that very mudrā, gathering them together in the manner of a pestle is the mudrā of the vajra pestle. From the mudrā of the vajra pestle, bending the middle and ring fingers of the right hand and raising the index finger in the manner of a club is the mudrā of the vajra club. Joining the middle finger to it as well and extending it is the mudrā of the vajra sword. From the mudrā of the vajra sword, bending the first joint is the mudrā of the wheel vajra. From the mudrā of the wheel vajra, bending the two index fingers behind the middle finger and joining them is the mudrā of the vajra iron arrow. From the mudrā of the vajra iron arrow, joining the ring finger to it as well is the mudrā of the single-pointed vajra. From the mudrā of the single-pointed vajra, raising and extending the middle finger and bending one of them in the shape of a noose is the mudrā of the vajra noose.

Likewise, gathering three fingers of the left hand together is the mudrā of the vajra tortoise. From the mudrā of the vajra tortoise, raising the index finger is the mudrā of the vajra that destroys all the machines of the asuras. From this very mudrā, extending two fingers and drawing in the index finger is the mudrā of the vajra that summons and paralyzes. Likewise, joining both hands with hollowed palms, embracing the two ring fingers with the two middle fingers, and joining the two thumbs and the two index fingers at the sides is the mudrā of the vajra abode. From that very mudrā:

Extending both little fingers
And raising the two thumbs:
This is the pestle with a slender waist,
Held to be unobstructed everywhere.

From this very mudrā, bending the two index fingers close beside it is the mudrā of the vajra-adorned limbs.

Then the bodhisattva, the great being Vajrapāṇi, in order to accomplish fully the maṇḍala of the Unfailing Vajra, also began to explain the mudrās of great secrecy, the limbs and those arising from the limbs. They are an easy means of accomplishing siddhis for the bodhisattvas who practice the conduct of bodhisattvas through the door of secret mantra and are blessed by the vajra. They know the conduct of all the buddhas, and they are the nonconceptual, manifest, perfect awakening. Apart from those blessed by Vajrasattva, those who have served the Victors of the past, those who have fully realized the Great Vehicle, and those blessed by Vajradhara who have heard or retained them, not even all the śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas can know them, let alone others, worldly people and non-Buddhists (tīrthikas), knowing or cognizing them.

Then Vajradhara gazed upon the maṇḍala of the entire assembly with the two eyes of great love and great compassion, blessed it with the blessing of the maṇḍala of the Unfailing Vajra, and said, "Sons of good family, there is a Dharma door called the Vast Maṇḍala of Mudrās Arising from the Wisdom That Understands the Tathāgata from a Mere Hint, which liberates the infinite realm of beings."

When he had said this, at that moment a light ray called Protecting All Beings, together with a retinue of many hundreds of light rays, came forth from between his eyebrows. Having come forth, it stirred the aspirations of all beings throughout all the oceans of world systems that extend as far as the realm of space, and blessed the realm of beings with the vajra blessing of the Three Jewels. Then it circled the bodhisattva Vajrapāṇi once more and entered the ūrṇā hair itself.

Then the entire assembly spoke with one voice: "Great being, this is the time; this is the moment. Therefore we shall bestow siddhis on the bodhisattvas who practice the conduct of bodhisattvas through the door of secret mantra. We shall bestow protection, perfect splendor and the rest."

Then Vajradhara spoke of the mudrās of the limbs, together with the great secret:

The wise one should first bind
the mudrā called samaya,
then call to mind the heart-essence of the buddhas
and move the right hand.

oṃ The wisdom mudrā of the perfect buddhas destroys the city of ignorance; it is called the mudrā of the setting out of vajra wisdom.

Raising the right hand,
if one moves it all around toward the upper direction,
the hostile will be driven away,
and even those who stir will be made to tremble.

Having formed it in the manner of a closed fist
and raised the right hand,
the yogin should place it at the crown of the head.
The syllable kha, with a garland of blazing light
and with a bindu as well,
should be set upon the top of the head,
with a garland of flame tongues blazing all around,
radiant everywhere in the ten directions.
The uṣṇīṣa mudrā, the great mudrā,
abides upon the uṣṇīṣa.

The mantra is: vajra ho khaṃ svāhā

With that same hand, the index finger raised,
lifting it, on both eyebrows
the mantrin should set the syllable ha
furnished with the double mark.

The mantra is: vajra ho tu ma svāhā This is the vajra eyebrow mudrā.

In the manner of raising the middle finger,
lifting that same hand,
one should set on both eyes the syllable ha
furnished with the double mark.

The mantra is: vajra ho hrīḥ svāhā This is the vajra eye mudrā.

Lifting that same hand,
the powerful syllable ha,
in the manner of raising the little finger,
should be set upon the vajra nose.

The mantra is: vajra ho ha svāhā This is the vajra nose mudrā.

In the manner of raising the little finger,
lifting that same hand,
the mantrin should set on both ears
the syllable he.

The mantra is: vajra ha ho svāhā This is the vajra ear mudrā.

Raising the thumb, with that same hand
lifted, on both cheeks
the one who keeps the vows should set
the syllable ha with the double mark.

The mantra is: vajra ho ho svāhā This is the vajra cheek mudrā.

The mantrin, joining middle finger and thumb,
lifting that same hand,
should set the syllable ha with the double mark
upon both lips.

The mantra is: vajra ho hūṃ svāhā This is the vajra lip mudrā.

The knower of mantra, raising the thumb,
lifting that same hand,
should set the syllable ha with a bindu
at the place of the tongue.

The mantra is: vajra ho haṃ svāhā This is the vajra tongue mudrā.

Lifting the left hand,
the mantrin should set on both breasts
the syllable ra,
furnished with the double mark.

The mantra is: vajra ha hor svāhā This is the vajra breast mudrā.

That same left hand
should be placed at the throat;
the syllable ra with the double mark
should be set there with the tip of the index finger.

The mantra is: vajra ho hri svāhā This is the vajra śrīvatsa mudrā.

Lifting that same hand,
the wise one should place it toward the armpits;
the syllable ra with a bindu
should be properly set there.

vajra ho ra ma svāhā This is the vajra armpit mudrā.

Lifting that same hand,
in the manner of the ring finger,
two syllables ra, one stacked upon the other,
should be set upon both hands.

vajra ho rā rā svāhā This is the vajra virtue mudrā.

Lifting that same hand,
the mantrin should set upon the heart,
just as it should be,
the syllable ra bearing the vowel sign o.

vajra ho ro svāhā This is the vajra heart mudrā.

Forming the right hand in the gesture of granting the supreme,
the wise one, with the index finger,
should set the syllable ba with the double mark
two finger-widths from the place of the heart.

vajra ho bha svāhā This is the vajra limb mudrā.

Lowering that same hand,
with the middle finger, two syllables pa,
furnished with the double mark,
should be set on the left flank.

vajra ho bhi bi svāhā This is the vajra flank mudrā.

Lifting that same hand,
the wise one, on the right flank,
should set the syllable ba with a bindu
with the little finger.

The mantra is: vajra ho paṃ svāhā This is the vajra mudrā of the right flank.

Lifting that same hand,
with thumb and middle finger together,
the syllable ba with a bindu,
marked with the vowel sign o, should be set at the navel.

The mantra is: vajra ho baṃ svāhā This is the vajra navel mudrā.

Lifting that same hand,
two finger-widths below the navel,
two syllables ba with a bindu
should be set with the tip of the index finger.

The mantra is: vajra ho baṃ baṃ svāhā This is the vajra mudrā of the lovely Moon.

Lifting that same hand,
the intelligent one should set at the waist,
in the manner of the middle finger,
the syllable ba furnished with the double mark.

The mantra is: vajra ho bhau svāhā This is the vajra waist mudrā.

Turning the left hand face down,
and laying the right upon it face up,
joining the two hands crosswise,
thumbs and little fingers to one another,
one forms the manner of a vajra binding.
Raising the index fingers, one circles from the left with all the fingers in the manner of putting on armor. This is the vajra armor mudrā. The mantra is: vajra haṃ ha svāhā

Opening the two thumbs,
one casts it all around toward the right side.
If it drives away even gods such as Brahmā,
what need is there to speak of the hostile-minded?
The supreme Victors have declared
it to be the vajra wheel mudrā.
By the way of setting this in place,
one becomes a supreme Victor.

The mantra is: vajra ho baṃ ba svāhā

Bending the index fingers inward,
set the rest as before.
Raising the middle fingers upward,
one should place it on the ground below.

The mantra is: vajra ho haṃ svāhā This is the vajra limb mudrā.

Knowing all of these,
one becomes firm in a vajra body.
If even obstructors are pacified by one's gaze,
what need is there to speak of harm from others?
By one's gaze all illnesses
and all the hostile are made to fear.
If one recites the mantra together with the mudrā,
gods and the rest come under one's power.

Then the disciple should be examined.
Through the rite and the act of seeing,
one should choose one whose limbs all bear good marks,
whose limbs are complete, and who is suitable.
Those of impaired sight and those who look askance,
the savage and dwarfs, should be rejected.
In particular, the gaze should be examined,
for the gaze accomplishes all aims.
Fierce, gentle and intermediate:
the gaze is taught to be of three kinds.
Expelling and killing, and likewise
drying up and bringing to ruin,
subjugating the savage
and cutting them down are taught as fierce.
The gentle gaze makes things gentle;
it brings all beings under control.
With a mind vast in accomplishment,
one of gentle gaze speaks pleasantly.
Endowed with compassion and skillful means,
with the mind of awakening and without fear,
if it tames the hosts of Māra, great in might,
and all who are untamed,
what need is there to speak of all obstructors and vināyakas
and of all the hostile?
Therefore, in every way,
one should examine the gentle gaze.
With a mind even toward friend and foe,
having wholly given up the acts to be performed,
the mantrin who abides in the intermediate,
if he is skilled in means,
by merely thinking it in his mind
will accomplish all that is needed.
Therefore, in every way,
apply this gaze.
To one whose gaze is fully pure
and whose mind likewise is wholly pure,
for the sake of vajra wisdom,
the one who holds the vows teaches the supreme.

Then the youthful Mañjuśrī said to the bodhisattva Vajrapāṇi, "Son of good family, as I understand the meaning of what you have explained, I think that the bodhisattva should purify the mind."

When he had said this, Vajrapāṇi said to the youthful Mañjuśrī, "Excellent, Mañjuśrī, excellent! You have spoken these words well, for the sake of purifying the mind. Therefore, Mañjuśrī, listen. I shall explain to you the Dharma door called the purification of mind, endowed with which the bodhisattvas who practice the conduct of bodhisattvas through the door of secret mantra attain the secret siddhi in the secret mantras.

"Mañjuśrī, in this regard, a son or daughter of good family who has seen the maṇḍala, who has generated the mind set on awakening, whose mind is endowed with compassion, who is skilled in means, and who is skilled in the teaching of the way of the letters of the door of secret mantra, should think thus: 'There is no mind apart from speech, and no speech apart from mind. There is no form of the deity apart from mind. Mind itself is speech and speech itself is mind; the form of the deity itself is mind, and speech itself is the form of the deity.' If one is thus convinced that they are not to be made separate, the mantrin attains the purification of mind. When one endowed with the purification of mind always, in every way, sees his own body and the form of the deity, his own speech and the speech of the deity, his own mind and the mind of the deity, as equal, then he is in meditative equipoise.

"When, always and in every respect,
the mantrin is in meditative equipoise,
then he enters the state
of the sameness of body and the rest.
For one who abides in the state of sameness,
however many movements of the limbs there are
and however many words are spoken,
so many are secret mantras and mudrās.
The mantrin is everywhere without foes;
he always abides unshaken in bliss.
He attains the rank of every lord
and dwells in the state where desires are fulfilled.
He is cool and free of torment,
restrained in a discipline endowed with patience."

Then the bodhisattva Śāntimati said to the bodhisattva Vajrapāṇi, "Son of good family, it is wonderful how greatly this Dharma discourse on the door of the purification of mind benefits the bodhisattvas who practice the conduct of bodhisattvas through the door of secret mantra! Son of good family, it is like the great earth, which benefits two-footed and four-footed beings. Son of good family, in the same way this Dharma door also benefits the bodhisattvas who practice the conduct of bodhisattvas through the door of secret mantra. Son of good family, those bodhisattvas who strive earnestly in this Dharma door have the good fortune for the state of accomplishing the siddhis of secret mantra."

Vajrapāṇi said, "Śāntimati, it is so; it is just as you have said. They are destined for unsurpassed, perfect and complete awakening. They have the good fortune to enter, to hear, and to uphold the great vajra maṇḍala."

Then the entire assembly said to the bodhisattva Vajrapāṇi, "Blessed One, those sons of good family who strive earnestly in this Dharma discourse on the door of the purification of mind are worthy of homage. All of us, gathered together with our servants and our retinues, will honor them with clothing and food. We will hold them as teachers, show them reverence, and make offerings to them. Whether they dwell in a town or in a hermitage, we will avert all harm. We will bring about what benefits them. As long as we have not fulfilled all the hopes of those sons or daughters of good family who strive constantly and without interruption, we will not fully awaken into unsurpassed, perfect and complete awakening."

Then the bodhisattva Vajrapāṇi said to the entire assembly, "Excellent, excellent! Friends, it is like this: one in whose hand the door of the purification of mind rests holds the awakening of the tathāgatas. That is to say, one who practices the door of secret mantra and holds perfect and complete awakening should be honored and revered; this alone is what is to be done."

Then at that time the bodhisattva, the great being Vajrapāṇi, in order to protect those who practice the conduct of bodhisattvas through the door of secret mantra, spoke the words of secret mantra of the infinitely pure state:

namaḥ sarva tathāgatebhyaḥ sarva mukhebhyaḥ sarva bhaya bhi kha tebhyaḥ sarvathā hūṃ ca pa li ni ka ke ka rakṣa rakṣa dā ke bhya darma ṣa tāṃ gi caṇḍo li ṣa pa do li tiratna ha ma ba yi ni ma ni sam ma ma hā ya na si svāhā

If any teacher of the Dharma recollects it three times by day and three times by night, no fear or harm of any kind will arise for him.

Likewise, joining the two hands, bending the two middle fingers and putting them inside is the vajra mudrā of subduing at the seat of awakening. From this mudrā, bending the first joints of the two ring fingers is the vajra mudrā of rousing Māra. From this mudrā, drawing the two middle fingers out again and placing them as before is the mudrā that terrifies the army of Māra. From that mudrā, placing the two ring fingers side by side, bending the two thumbs and putting them inside is the mudrā that crushes the hosts of Māra. From that mudrā, drawing out the two thumbs, bending the two index fingers and putting them inside is the mudrā that tames Māra. The mantra is:

namaḥ sarva tathāgatebhyaḥ sarva mukhebhyaḥ sarvathā he he svāhā

From this mudrā, pressing the right thumb with the left thumb is the mudrā that cuts down all grahas. The mantra is:

namaḥ sarva tathāgatebhyaḥ sarva mukhebhyaḥ sarvathā hi svāhā

From this mudrā, pressing the left thumb with the right index finger is the mudrā that cuts down all rākṣasas. The mantra is:

namaḥ sarva tathāgatebhyaḥ sarva mukhebhyaḥ sarvathā te he re ṭe svāhā

From this mudrā, rubbing the palm of the right hand with the ring finger of the left hand is the mudrā that crushes the hearts of all ḍākinīs. The mantra is:

namaḥ sarva tathāgatebhyaḥ sarva mukhebhyaḥ sarvathā he raṅgi ṇi he he svāhā

From this mudrā, forming it as a cupped handful, with the two index fingers and the two thumbs shaped like a conch, is the vajra conch mudrā; it crushes the hearts of all nāgas. The mantra is:

namaḥ sarva tathāgatebhyaḥ sarva mukhebhyaḥ sarvathā jaṃ gi ri phutta re re svāhā

Likewise, joining the two hands and crossing the two middle fingers is the mudrā that cuts down all yakṣas. The mantra is:

namaḥ sarva tathāgatebhyaḥ sarva mukhebhyaḥ sarvathā jiṃ gi ri ṇi hūṃ na de ke svāhā

From this mudrā, putting the two little fingers inside and placing the two middle fingers as before is the mudrā that cuts down the gandharvas. The mantra is:

namaḥ sarva tathāgatebhyaḥ sarva mukhebhyaḥ sarvathā he ri ge bhyaḥ svāhā

From this mudrā, forming the two index fingers as if embracing is the mudrā that utterly crushes the asuras. The mantra is:

namaḥ sarva tathāgatebhyaḥ sarva mukhebhyaḥ sarvathā hūṃ hūṃ phaṭ phaṭ

From this mudrā, embracing and raising the two thumbs and extending the remaining fingers is the mudrā by which all garuḍas perform any task whatever, like servants. The mantra is:

namaḥ sarva tathāgatebhyaḥ sarva mukhebhyaḥ sarvathā ha ri piṅgo li mu khi svāhā

Likewise, joining the two hands, turning the left hand face up and the right face down, and touching the palm of the left hand with the ring finger is the mudrā that cuts down the kiṃnaras. The mantra is:

namaḥ sarva tathāgatebhyaḥ sarva mukhebhyaḥ sarvathā ja ya ja ya ni svāhā

From this mudrā, touching the palm of that same hand with the index finger is the mudrā that cuts down the great mahoragas. The mantra is:

namaḥ sarva tathāgatebhyaḥ sarva mukhebhyaḥ sarvathā ga ra li ni hūṃ ka ri ṇi svāhā

Likewise, joining the two hands and crossing the two hands is the mudrā that wards off all the hostile. The mantra is:

namaḥ sarva tathāgatebhyaḥ sarva mukhebhyaḥ sarvathā he caṇḍi he ri bi ke svāhā

With those same hands, interlacing the fingers each with the other and placing the two hands as before is the mudrā that burns the hearts of all rākṣasas. The mantra is:

namaḥ sarva tathāgatebhyaḥ sarva mukhebhyaḥ sarvathā keṅ ka ri ṇi jhaṃ ka ri ṇi ke svāhā

From this mudrā, bending the two thumbs slightly is the mudrā that bewilders the hearts of all rākṣasas. The mantra is:

namaḥ sarva tathāgatebhyaḥ sarva mukhebhyaḥ sarvathā kar na ji te bi kha la mu ki svāhā

From this mudrā, opening the two index fingers and placing the thumbs as before is the mudrā that tames the piśācas; so the supreme Victors have always taught. The mantra is:

namaḥ sarva tathāgatebhyaḥ sarva mukhebhyaḥ sarvathā mi ci svāhā

From this mudrā, opening the two middle fingers and placing the two index fingers as before is the mudrā that bewilders the hearts of all piśācīs. The mantra is:

namaḥ sarva tathāgatebhyaḥ sarva mukhebhyaḥ sarvathā pi śā ci ni svāhā

From this mudrā, opening the two ring fingers as well and placing the two middle fingers as before is the mudrā that brings all bhūtas under control. The mantra is:

namaḥ sarva tathāgatebhyaḥ sarva mukhebhyaḥ sarvathā guṃ guṃ svāhā

From this mudrā, opening the two little fingers as well and placing the two ring fingers as before is the mudrā that summons all bhūtas. The mantra is:

namaḥ sarva tathāgatebhyaḥ sarva mukhebhyaḥ sarvathā gu mi gu mi hi li ge svāhā

From this mudrā, extending the left thumb and placing the rest as before is the mudrā that terrifies all shadow-seizing spirits. The mantra is:

namaḥ sarva tathāgatebhyaḥ sarva mukhebhyaḥ sarvathā chā yā li svāhā

Likewise, extending the left thumb and placing the right hand as before is the mudrā that frightens all shadow-seizing spirits. The mantra is:

namaḥ sarva tathāgatebhyaḥ sarva mukhebhyaḥ sarvathā jo la ni ja ti ke svāhā

From this mudrā, opening the two index fingers, bending them so that they cross, and placing them on top of the two thumbs is the mudrā that destroys all apasmāras. The mantra is:

namaḥ sarva tathāgatebhyaḥ sarva mukhebhyaḥ sarvathā laṃ bod ra ṇī bhri bhri śi ni svāhā

From this mudrā, opening and raising all the fingers is the mudrā that terrifies all female apasmāras.

The mantra is:

namaḥ sarva tathāgatebhyaḥ sarva mukhebhyaḥ sarvathā be ratni ulka mu gi ci phu ri re svāhā

Likewise, bending all the fingers and putting them inside, and raising the two thumbs: this is the mudrā that dries up the hearts of the kṛtyās. The mantra is:

namaḥ sarva tathāgatebhyaḥ sarva mukhebhyaḥ sarvathā khi khi ri hṛ da ya bi dā ra ṇi svāhā

From this mudrā, bending the two thumbs and putting them inside, and raising the two index fingers: this is the mudrā that binds fast the hearts of all kṛtyās. The mantra is:

namaḥ sarva tathāgatebhyaḥ sarva mukhebhyaḥ sarvathā bis ri ṇi svāhā

Then again Vajrapāṇi, for the sake of those bodhisattvas who practice the conduct of bodhisattvas through the door of secret mantra, who possess the power of the ten powers, and who arise from vajra wisdom, spoke the door that teaches the vast secret of the mudrās that arise from the heart-essence of all the tathāgatas.

First one pays homage to all the buddhas, makes the mind of awakening manifest, and pervades the realm of beings with the eye of great love and great compassion. Then, anointing the hands with perfume and facing east or north, one should properly bind the mudrā.

Joining the two hands in a cupped handful, praising, and setting them down is the mudrā of the Tathāgata Ratnaketu. The mantra is:

namaḥ sarva tathāgatebhyaḥ sarva mukhebhyaḥ sarvathā tri ratna nirjāta hūṃ hūṃ svāhā

From this mudrā, bending the two thumbs and placing them against the sides is the mudrā of the Tathāgata Saṃkusumitarājendra. The mantra is:

namaḥ sarva tathāgatebhyaḥ sarva mukhebhyaḥ sarvathā jñāna sambhava svāhā

From this mudrā, bending the two index fingers in the manner of a vajra and placing them directly against the sides of the middle fingers is the mudrā of the Blessed One Amitābha. The mantra is:

namaḥ sarva tathāgatebhyaḥ sarva mukhebhyaḥ sarvathā a mi todbha be jñā na pa ri ṇi svāhā

From this mudrā, bending the two index fingers and making their tips touch the middle fingers is the mudrā of Akṣobhya. The mantra is:

namaḥ sarva tathāgatebhyaḥ sarva mukhebhyaḥ sarvathā bhi ga da jvā jñā na saṃ bha va svāhā

Clenching both hands into fists, with the two index fingers embracing the two thumbs, is the mudrā of the Blessed One Vipaśyin. The mantra is:

namaḥ sarva tathāgatebhyaḥ sarva mukhebhyaḥ sarvathā ka na bi cu ti ta jñā no da ya svāhā

From this mudrā, raising the two middle fingers side by side is the mudrā of the Blessed One, the Tathāgata Śikhin. The mantra is:

namaḥ sarva tathāgatibhyaḥ sarva mukhebhyaḥ sarvathā jñā na śi khi saṃ bha va svāhā

Likewise, clenching the fists as before and raising the two ring fingers side by side is the mudrā of the Blessed One Viśvabhū. The mantra is:

namaḥ sarva tathāgatebhyaḥ sarva mukhebhyaḥ sarvathā bi śuddha ga ta jñā na bi de svāhā

Clenching the fists in that way as before and raising the two little fingers side by side is the mudrā of the Blessed One Krakucchanda. The mantra is:

namaḥ sarva tathāgatebhyaḥ sarva mukhebhyaḥ sarvathā ji ma svāhā

Likewise, clenching the fists and bending the two thumbs and the two index fingers inward is the mudrā of the head of the Blessed One, the great Cakravartin. The mantra is:

namaḥ sarva tathāgatebhyaḥ sarva mukhebhyaḥ sarvathā kha tra mi svāhā

From this mudrā, drawing out the two middle fingers and raising them side by side is the mudrā of the uṣṇīṣa of the Blessed One, the great Cakravartin. The mantra is:

namaḥ sarva tathāgatebhyaḥ sarva mukhebhyaḥ sarvathā jāṃ

From this mudrā, drawing out the two index fingers, bending their first joints, and joining them to that same place is the mudrā of the eyes. The mantra is:

namaḥ sarva tathāgatebhyaḥ sarva mukhebhyaḥ sarvathā dri

From this mudrā, bending the two thumbs slightly, turning them toward the two index fingers, and placing the rest as before is the mudrā of the wheel of the Blessed One. The mantra is:

namaḥ sarva tathāgatebhyaḥ sarva mukhebhyaḥ sarvathā aṃ

As before, clenching both hands into fists, spreading the two index fingers outward and extending them with the first joints bent, and spreading the two thumbs outward and extending them, is the mudrā of the śrīvatsa of the Blessed One. The mantra is:

namaḥ sarva tathāgatebhyaḥ sarva mukhebhyaḥ sarvathā śrīḥ

From this mudrā, spreading and extending the two middle fingers and putting the two thumbs inside is the mudrā of the navel of the Blessed One. The mantra is:

namaḥ sarva tathāgatebhyaḥ sarva mukhebhyaḥ sarvathā hriṃ

As before, clenching both hands into fists, drawing out the two little fingers and spreading them: this is the mudrā of the feet of the Blessed One. The mantra is:

namaḥ sarva tathāgatebhyaḥ sarva mukhebhyaḥ sarvathā hraṃ

These are the mudrās of the Blessed One, the great Cakravartin; they accomplish every aim that is needed.

Whoever, through inner purity,
holds these mudrās,
that mantrin will become
free of evil; of this there is no doubt.
All the Vajradharas,
and the bodhisattvas,
and all the gods of great miraculous power
protect that intelligent one.
Therefore, in every way,
one should revere the great-souled one.
If one constantly recites the vidyā-mantra,
siddhi will not be difficult for him.

The mantras spoken by the Victors
will be accomplished in this very life.
Whatever worldly mantras there are,
they all come under his power;
saying, "What command shall we carry out?"
they become his retinue on his right and left.
Nonhuman divine maidens too
come under his power and remain with him.
When the mantrin, abiding in the way of mantra,
comes to cast off his body,
then he will be born
in the supreme abode of the Vaśavartin gods.
Where the powerful Blessed One,
the great Vairocana, dwells,
there the son of the Victors, as he wishes,
will take birth.
Until he attains awakening,
his conduct will not decline.
All the Vajradharas
bless him in every way.

Likewise, joining both hands, putting the two ring fingers inside, bending the first joints of the two index fingers, placing the two middle fingers side by side so that they are kept apart by about the width of a barleycorn, and placing the two thumbs beside them is the mudrā of the Great Fierce Vajra. The mantra is:

namaḥ sarva tathāgatebhyaḥ sarva mukhebhyaḥ sarvathā caṇḍa mahā ro ṣa ṇa hūṃ

From that mudrā, embracing the raised middle finger with the right index finger: this is the mudrā of the head of the Great Fierce Vajra. The mantra is:

namaḥ sarva tathāgatebhyaḥ sarva mukhebhyaḥ sarvathā maṃ

Embracing the raised middle finger with the left index finger, opening the right index finger, and placing it with its first joint bent as before is the mudrā of the uṣṇīṣa of the Great Fierce Vajra. The mantra is:

namaḥ sarva tathāgatebhyaḥ sarva mukhebhyaḥ sarvathā jī

Likewise, embracing the raised middle finger with the two index fingers is the mudrā of the armor of the Great Fierce Vajra. The mantra is:

namaḥ sarva tathāgatebhyaḥ sarva mukhebhyaḥ sarvathā ā

Likewise, opening the two index fingers and extending them in the manner of flashing lightning is the mudrā of the great blazing vajra flame of the Fierce Vajra. The mantra is:

namaḥ sarva tathāgatebhyaḥ sarva mukhebhyaḥ sarva mukhebhyaḥ sarvathā kśaṃ

From this mudrā, bending the right index finger and placing it beside the thumb is the mudrā of the forehead of the Great Fierce Vajra. The mantra is:

namaḥ sarva tathāgatebhyaḥ sarva mukhebhyaḥ sarvathā ni laṃ ba ti ka ti svāhā

Likewise, placing the left index finger beside the thumb, bending the first joint of the raised middle finger and lowering it slightly is the mudrā of the breasts of the Great Fierce Vajra. The mantra is:

namaḥ sarva tathāgatebhyaḥ sarva mukhebhyaḥ sarvathā ha sa ha sa dha ka dha ka ca ra ca ra haṃ pra caṇḍa pajra svāhā

Likewise, from the breast mudrā, bending the two index fingers, spreading and extending them, and putting the two little fingers inside is the mudrā of the śrīvatsa of the Great Fierce Vajra. The mantra is:

namaḥ sarva tathāgatebhyaḥ sarva mukhebhyaḥ sarvathā pi phu la vajra caṇḍa svāhā

From this mudrā, drawing out the little fingers and placing them as before is the mudrā of the feet of the Great Fierce Vajra. The mantra is:

namaḥ sarva tathāgatebhyaḥ sarva mukhebhyaḥ sarvathā bi ci tra ga ti svāhā

Thus these vast mudrās are blessed by all the tathāgatas and abide in manifold forms. They are applied to the many realms of beings in the many world systems. Their power is unobstructed in the three times. They arise from the realization of the dharmadhātu. Blessed by all the tathāgatas, they cut off the destinies of gods, nāgas, yakṣas, gandharvas, asuras, garuḍas, kinnaras, great mahoragas, humans and nonhumans. They bring about the attainment of the realization of the tathāgatas. They establish the display that reveals the array of the body, speech and mind of the tathāgatas. They bring wishes to complete fulfillment just as they are intended. They accomplish the array of the maṇḍala of the Unfailing Vajra. They are, without exception, the eye of the bodhisattvas who practice the conduct of bodhisattvas through the door of secret mantra, and of the world with its gods, humans and asuras.

This completes the vast mudrās, as many as there are, from the array of the great maṇḍala of the Empowerment of Vajrapāṇi, from the teaching of the outcome that accords with the past conduct of the tathāgatas, and from the state of miraculous display.

The Noble Empowerment of Vajrapāṇi, section ten.

Then again Vajrapāṇi entered the samādhi called the Emanated Array That Defeats All Māras.

As soon as the bodhisattva Vajrapāṇi had entered absorption, all the tathāgatas of the ten directions stretched out their right hands, stroked Vajrapāṇi's head, and gave their approval, saying "Excellent!":

You who benefit all sentient beings,
Great hero, excellent, excellent!
For the sake of the vajra stances
That arise from the practice of secret mantra,
You have entered this samādhi
That terrifies hostile beings.
The sequence of the rite of the vajra stances,
Great being, you shall teach.
The stances of vajra arising
Are common to all the kings of mantra:
Sitting and going,
Rising and scattering,
Summoning and stretching out,
Binding with the feet and drawing in,
Shifting and setting down,
Seizing with a clenched fist,
Tormenting and smashing with the vajra,
Nailing down with the vajra mudrā,
Leaping like a lion,
Threatening with hūṃ,
Ears drawn up and lip pressed down,
Gazing with a wrathful frown upon the brow,
Making the eyebrows move,
Likewise opening both eyes wide,
Distinguishing the various stances,
Joining the two hands,
Likewise moving the hands,
Leaping with both feet,
And also displaying the body
In particular with a haughty bearing,
Cutting, chopping and laughing,
Devouring and laughing aloud,
Resounding all around and roaring forth,
Acting fiercely and binding,
Crushing all the hostile,
Paralyzing and stupefying,
Leaping upward and striking,
Graceful stances and whirling,
The preliminary practice at the outset of accomplishment,
Likewise the approach to the deity,
The mantrin's maṇḍala,
Whatever kinds of accomplishments there are,
Protecting and guarding in every way
The realm of sentient beings,
All these, O hero,
And whatever else the mind desires,
And likewise restoring again
Those who are possessed of hostile minds:
All these, at length,
Proclaim, wishing for the liberation of beings!

Then, so that victory might be won by means of the stances, those tathāgatas spoke this secret mantra:

namaḥ sarvatathāgatebhyaḥ sarvamukhebhyaḥ sarvathā hūṃ hūṃ hūṃ phaṭ phaṭ phaṭ caṇḍavajraprajvalitamūrti svāhā

Then Vajrapāṇi, having heard this secret mantra, was delighted and pleased, and with a mind full of joy he looked over the entire assembly and said, "O friends, listen! So that the teaching of the Teacher may long endure, from this day forward protect any beings who have gone for refuge to the Buddha, the Dharma and the Saṅgha, and those bodhisattvas who engage in the conduct of secret mantra and strive in the way of secret mantra. Honor them! Hold them in esteem! Make offerings to them!"

Then that entire assembly spoke with one voice, saying, "We shall do as the Lord of the Dharma has commanded," and sat in silence.

Then Vajrapāṇi again addressed the circle of the entire assembly: "Sons of noble family, if in time to come anyone who strives to accomplish the protection of the maṇḍala of the past conduct of the tathāgatas transgresses the commitments of the bodhisattvas who practice the conduct of bodhisattvas through the door of secret mantra, the fierce king of knowledge will split his head into seven pieces with a vajra that blazes, flares, spreads in every direction and burns intensely."

Then the bodhisattva, the great being Vajrapāṇi, spoke thus to the bodhisattva Śāntimati: "Śāntimati, listen as I explain the characteristics of the stances taught by the buddhas of the past.

Having taken a fitting stance
And bowed to the tathāgatas,
The mantrin, facing east,
Stands and places his joined palms at the crown.
With supreme grace befitting the secret mantra
He dances in the manner of the vajra stance,
And, lowering his joined palms from the crown,
In the manner of placing them at the heart,
He should turn toward all the directions.
Holding the vajra fist,
He raises the right hand
And places the left behind the waist.
Lifting the right foot,
He should bend the right one well;
Raising the left up high,
He should threaten all the hostile.
He stretches out the right leg,
Both hands holding the vajra fist,
Draws himself up with a wrathful hūṃ,
And strikes with the vajra blow.
Turning in the vajra dance,
He bends the left leg,
And likewise in the four directions
He treads the vajra's trampling gait.
Joining the vajra palms,
He places them at the waist.
Standing in the posture with heels together,
He should cast his eyes downward.
When he strikes with the vajra dagger,
The seven underworlds will quake.
With a hūṃ like rolling thunder,
Like the blazing Vajradhara,
He should thrust the vajra upward
And join his palms behind his back.
Then, performing the vajra dance,
He places it at the navel.
Having stood in such a posture,
One well trained in swift movement sets it down.
However many weapon mudrās there are
Of the buddhas and bodhisattvas,
Of the wrathful ones, the attendants and the maidservants,
The vajra, the club and the cudgel,
The short spear, the great arrow and the wheel,
The single-pointed vajra, the pestle and the rest,
And whatever messengers there are, male and female,
They turn back the obstructors and the like.
Whatever other hosts of mudrās have been taught
For the destruction of obstructors,
By those to whom this maṇḍala
And its general rite have been shown in full,
The trained mantrin utterly destroys
The gods, the asuras and the guhyakas.
Even if, to obstruct the maṇḍala,
From those reaching up to Brahmā's pillar
Down to the gods who dwell upon the earth,
All gathered together and came,
The mantrin who knows the rite of the stances,
By means of hūṃ,
Will scatter them all
Like tufts of cotton scattered by the wind.
Becoming the fire of the blazing vajra,
Rising up like the fire at the end of the eon,
With vajra feet
He destroys everything with the vajra mudrā.
With the vajra's trampling,
Having crushed them with the vajra mudrā,
If the mantrin can lay waste the three worlds,
What need is there to speak of obstructors and vināyakas?

This stance of the great vajra mudrā
Is the general one.
If it accomplishes even aims beyond accomplishing,
What need to speak of what comes from the detailed rite?
As for the supreme vajra arising,
Having first performed the binding,
One should then bind the other arisings as well
From the supreme vajra knowledge.
From this come the secret mantras
And all the kinds of stances.
Therefore, in every way,
The mantrin should train with diligence.

This is the root stance of the great commitment, vajra arising.

Seated in the vajra arising,
Like a lion he overpowers the adversary;
With the vajra mudrā he destroys all.
He should stretch out the vajra foot,
Proclaiming the sound of hūṃ.
Having crushed them with the vajra mudrā,
If even all the lands are made to quake,
What need is there to speak of guhyakas and nāgas?
When he has leapt in the manner of a lion,
Whatever obstructors have forms like lions,
Elephants, chariots, monkeys and horses,
And whatever other four-footed ones there are,
Will then flee in all directions
Or else die, without a doubt.
Even obstructors sent by Brahmā
Are swiftly destroyed in that instant.

This is the stance of overpowering the adversary like a lion.

Again seated in the vajra arising,
In the manner of crossed feet,
He should place both vajra fists
At the waist.
Uttering hūṃ with the sound of clearing the throat,
He dances with the vajra dance.
Lifting the left foot,
He plants its tip upon the ground;
Lifting the left foot,
With the hand raised holding the vajra,
And placing the left hand at the heart,
The mantrin turns like a wheel.
Transformed into the fire of the blazing vajra,
Possessed of the eyes of the vajra sun,
With the vajra sound of clearing the throat
He should fill all the directions completely.
Focusing on the abode of the obstructors,
Swiftly, with the vajra stride,
In the position with the right leg extended,
He destroys all with the vajra mudrā.
Likewise the directions and the intermediate directions
He should thoroughly destroy on every side.
The great and terrible hosts of Māra,
And all that arise from every direction,
Applying the stride with the right extended,
He destroys all with the vajra mudrā.
If all the three worlds are made to quake,
What need is there to speak of obstructors and vināyakas?
On seeing the vajra mudrā,
All the obstructors and vināyakas
And the rākṣasas of terrible form
Flee in every direction.

This is the vajra right-extended stance.

Seated in the vajra arising,
He joins the vajra palms at the crown.
Circling the hands that hold the vajra,
He should pervade everything like clouds.
Dancing with the vajra dance,
He should press his lip with his teeth.
He should raise the left fist
And place the right at the heart.
Lifting the left foot,
He should twist it toward the right.
Uttering hūṃ like rolling thunder,
Pressing down with the foot in the left-extended stance,
He should strike with the vajra foot,
And with the right he should pierce.
When the vajra foot strikes
The underworld, it makes everything quake.
The nāgas who dwell beneath the earth,
With the guhyakas, asuras and rākṣasas,
All saying, "What is this shaking?",
Their minds seized with amazement,
With amazement in their hearts,
Ask one another,
"Oh, what will become of all of us?
Surely the eon has not come to its end?"
Moving once more from that place,
Possessed of blazing vajra eyes,
Uttering hūṃ with the vajra mudrā,
He should strike with the vajra foot.
All the nāgas and guhyakas,
And whatever others dwell upon the earth,
All gather at the place
Where the mantra holder sits,
And, utterly scorched by the blazing vajra,
They await his command in full:
"Lord, what is it you seek?
Quickly grant us your command!
Is it an elixir, or mercury,
Or the substances of eye ointment?
Or yakṣas, male and female, as your subjects?
Or do you wish for kingship?
Or do you wish for the door to fortune?
Or if you wish for other lands,
Tormented as we are by the blazing vajra,
We shall fulfill all that you wish."
The mantrin should accept some of these
And show loving-kindness toward them.
Performing the vajra dance well,
He should withdraw that mudrā,
And by the procedure seen in the rite
He should set them in the vajra place.
Otherwise the guhyakas
And all the rākṣasas will do harm:
Watching for an opening and seeking faults,
Those tormented ones will do harm,
And, having found an opportunity,
They will harm the mantrin.
Therefore, in every way,
The practitioner endowed with intelligence,
Having come to know the vajra place,
Should apply the practice of the stances.

This is the stance of the left-extended leg.

Seated in the vajra arising,
He should join his palms at the forehead.
Having performed the vajra dance,
He should place his palms at the heart.
Having taken the stance of Brahmā,
He should open out the lotus palms.
Holding both hands in fists,
He should stretch the right one upward,
And, placing the left one at the heart,
Draw himself up in wrath with hūṃ and phaṭ.
Having pressed his lip with his teeth,
Possessed of the eyes of the vajra sun,
His face furrowed in a wrathful frown,
In the manner of one who dances
He should draw in the right foot,
And likewise draw in the left.
With the mudrā of the raised vajra
He should lift his hand upward.
Looking upward, fiercely
He should strike with the vajra mudrā.
When he strikes with the vajra mudrā,
All, with Brahmā and with Śakra,
Together with their abodes, are shaken.
They look at one another
And all say to one another,
"Friends, is the eon in turmoil?
Or has some great being appeared?
Or has a buddha come forth?
What is it?"
Utterly bewildered, they gaze
At one another and in the ten directions.
Since they are tormented by the blazing vajra,
They go from that place
And await his command, saying,
"Speak, Vajra Bearer: what is your wish?
We have committed no offense,
Nor have we acted against your command.
Tormented by the blazing vajra,
Quickly grant us your command!"
The vajra holder gazes constantly
At the vajra without blinking.
Those other ordinary gods as well,
Brought to a hundred straits by the blazing vajra,
Say to the one possessed of the supreme vajra,
"Take whatever is in your heart!
Or if you wish for kingship,
Or likewise wish for humans and gods,
Or for proud daughters of the gods,
Or wish for the pleasures of the gods, as your mind desires,
Whatever your heart desires,
All of that we shall offer."
Then the many gods of great miraculous power,
Their minds filled with delight,
Tormented by the blazing vajra,
Swiftly make offerings to the practitioner.
Then, with a mind of loving-kindness,
The mantrin should look upon them and let them go.
Otherwise those tormented ones,
Finding an opening, will do harm.

Again seated in the vajra arising,
He should perform the vajra dance intently.
Joining his palms at the crown,
He should open them out toward all the directions.
In the manner of shifting the feet,
He lifts the right, and likewise the left.
In the manner of the vajra fist
He should raise the right hand upward,
And, placing the left at the heart,
Drawing in and moving the eyebrows,
When he looks at the obstructors with his eyes
And urges himself on with hūṃ,
Even if it were Brahmā himself,
Even if it were Viṣṇu himself,
Even if it were fierce Śakra himself,
His gaze swiftly makes them afraid.
If he wishes to bring Viṣṇu and the fierce ones
Under his own power,
In the manner of moving all around,
With the eyes of the vajra sun,
In the manner of the feet joined in method,
He should strike with the vajra mudrā.
When he strikes with the vajra mudrā,
All the lands shake violently.
Then Śakra and the gods,
And the asuras, rākṣasas and guhyakas,
Fall from their seats of their own accord
And faint away, unconscious.
Then Viṣṇu and the fierce ones,
Their minds stunned,
Go with joined palms to where
The one endowed with the great vajra yoga is,
Saying, "Though we have committed no offense,
Your mantra torments us terribly."
All those tormented gods
Lose their composure upon their seats,
And sit gazing with reverence for his command,
Saying, "Tell us how, and what, we are to do."
When he has shown loving-kindness to all
And taken up a single intention in his heart,
At that very moment they,
Delighted and pleased, grant it.
The mantrin, resting in equipoise,
Releases them with the king of mudrās,
And in the manner of shifting the feet
He should set himself in the vajra place.
For all the vajra holders,
The vajra place is defined from the waist.

Seated in the vajra arising,
He stands firm in the posture of Mahendra.
Having joined his palms at the crown,
He should open them out toward all the directions.
In the manner of shifting the feet,
Holding the vajra fist,
With the hand of one who dances
He should threaten the forms of the obstructors.
Likewise with the right and with the left
He should do so until they leave their place.
If the great, fiercely hostile obstructors,
Though threatened, do not go away,
He should quickly take a vajra stride,
Move his hands all about above him,
Slap both hands upon the ground,
And utter hūṃ like rolling thunder.
The wise one of secret mantra, bending his legs,
Turns his body in every way.
Then, in a hostile manner,
Stretching out the right vajra hand,
With the mudrā of the vajra hook
He should summon all the obstructors.
In the manner of right and left,
Performing the action of Brahmā with each,
Urging them on fiercely with hūṃs like rolling thunder
And with phaṭs,
With the mudrā of the vajra torment
He should torment all the obstructors.
Apart from the state of the Sugata,
If the vajra bearer, the wheel bearer,
Or any other child of the buddhas
Pays no heed to his own body,
Gods, gandharvas, guhyakas,
And whatever nāgas there may be
Are scattered like tufts of cotton by the wind:
When he utters hūṃ they grow afraid, needless to say.
What need is there to speak of what befalls
Asuras, savage rākṣasas,
Pretas, kumbhāṇḍas and servants,
Māra and the messengers of Māra?
Resting in equipoise in a mind of loving-kindness,
And resting in equipoise in compassion as well,
The mantrin, mindful of the mind of awakening,
Should begin to withdraw the mudrā.
For the sake of some slight fault
One should not harm the obstructors.
Only when one knows them to be extremely hostile
May one harm the obstructors.
Toward the extremely hostile and fierce
One should not act with gentleness alone.

If one acts with gentleness alone,
At that very moment they do harm.
First one should apply gentle means;
The second time, strive to divide them;
The third time, taking up a welcome offering,
And holding flowers in cupped hands,
One should then make offerings to the obstructors.
When one gives the torma that benefits all bhūtas,
One should speak these words:
"Since I have honored you today,
Go, great obstructor, be on your way!
From now on, do not act like this!
I have seen your strength and prowess:
You are able to obstruct.
You are an emanation of the gods;
It is not that you cannot obstruct.
Since you had not received offerings before,
And today I have made you offerings,
Now take your offerings,
Lord of obstructors, and go!" So one should say.
If he remains yet does not obstruct,
That is good conduct.
But if the obstructor, though fit to be honored
By the mantrin, goes on obstructing,
One should say, "May the buddhas of the ten directions,
Who have attained mastery, the supreme among humans,
Take note! Though I apply the practice
To punish the obstructor, I commit no fault.
I do not act against the command
Of the buddhas and the buddhas' children,
The bodhisattvas."
To the gods, asuras and guhyakas,
To the gandharvas of all ten directions,
And to whatever planets and kiṃnaras there are,
To the protectors and the great protectors,
One should proclaim the harm done by the obstructor:
"If it is asked, 'Protect even a little!',
Then swiftly give full protection!"
If, having heard this proclamation,
The one who obstructs becomes peaceful,
One should then make offerings
To the gods of all the directions.
If he does not stay so,
One should strongly urge the buddhas and the rest
With flowers, joined palms and welcome offerings,
And punish him.
Toward the fierce one of extremely hostile mind,
Who goes against the command
And violently robs others of their lives,
The buddhas too do not turn away,
Knowing that it benefits him, thinking,
"Otherwise, for many tens of millions of eons
He would be born in the hells
And undergo much suffering."
Just as a physician heals
The illnesses of sentient beings,
So I incur no fault by tormenting him;
The wise have not rejected this.
Thus, with the mind in equipoise
And a heart possessed of compassion,
Making the mind of awakening firm,
One undertakes to punish him.
Just as a skilled physician
Does not abandon one who is ailing,
So too one skilled in method and wisdom,
If, though skilled, he does not act, is at fault.
Just as an unskilled physician,
Lacking method and wisdom,
Abandons the patient
And does not perform the rite of healing,
So too, one devoid of knowledge,
Even if he strives to heal,
Lacking method and wisdom,
Is not praised by the buddhas.
When a physician gives medicine
To each of the sick,
Even if it is hot, bitter or astringent,
The world does not revile him.
In the same way, by the particulars of discipline,
One should heal each as is fitting.
The buddhas do not scorn or revile
Hostile sentient beings.
In times gone by, long ago,
There appeared a most wondrous Victor.
In the scriptures of the tathāgatas
The way of the world is described thus:
At that time there arose
All beings of fierce and extremely hostile mind.
Seeing them to be extremely fierce,
He did not employ gentleness alone.
The Tathāgata Vajrasattva,
Courageous in wisdom and method,
His courage of compassion turned to wrath,
Benefiting sentient beings,
Then, by overpowering the adversary,
Overpowered many realms of beings.
By the force of stern means free of evil,
One should bring them all under control.
By the method of the family of overpowering the adversary,
As he aspired to awakening,
He brought the many realms of beings,
All of them, under his control.
The great vajra master
Likewise subdues the vināyakas and obstructors.
If he meditates, mindful of the mind of awakening,
He is not stained by fault.
Therefore the mantrin, in great wrath,
With power like the fire at the end of the eon,
Should cut down all the obstructors
With the mudrā of the vajra sword.
With that very manner of the feet,
Dancing with the vajra dance,
Stretching out the left foot, he casts it down;
Relying on the position with the right foot extended,
Striking at the place of the waist,
He should put the sword into its sheath.
In a manner that does not press down,
Clenching the hand forcefully to the ground,
In the manner of shifting the feet,
He holds it as if drawing the sword.
To set it in the form of the sword,
He should put the index and middle fingers together.
Then, with the left hand,
To make the sword's sheath,
With the little finger, the ring finger
And the thumb he should make the sheath of the sword.
Then with the right foot
Drawing the form of the obstructor on the ground,
Pressing his lip with his teeth in wrath,
Blazing with the sun of his own vajra,
He presses the head down with his foot,
And having drawn the sword with his hand,
With the vajra stride
He moves all about in the manner of a dance,
And with the mudrā of the vajra sword
He should cut down all the obstructors.
Whomever that vajra bearer strikes down,
He has no regard for that one's life.
Then they will become peaceful,
Or else die, without a doubt.
Even an obstructor equal to Śakra
Will be destroyed at that very moment.
Then the great vajra master,
Possessed of a mind of compassion and tender love,
Should show him tender love
So that he may be born again.

This is the stance of the vajra sword.

Then the great vajra master
Performs the act of chopping with the vajra:
With the mudrā of the vajra wheel
He chops up all who intend harm.
Seated in the vajra arising,
Taking up the vajra's might,
In the manner of lifting and setting down the feet
He should perform the vajra dance intently.
In the sequence of spreading out the wheel,
Joining the lotus palms,
Having set the feet apart,
He should hurl the wheel upon the ground.
Remaining in the posture with heels together,
Uttering hūṃ like rolling thunder,
In the manner of clapping the palms
He should strike the ground with the wheel.
Reversing it and doing so again,
In the manner of joining the hands,
Making them like a wheel,
He should hurl it upward and destroy.
Drawing it back, pressing the head down with his foot,
He should strike at the obstructors.
Then either they will become utterly peaceful
Or else they will die, without a doubt.
