Mañjuśrī, the place where this teaching of the maṇḍala of the great dhāraṇī-mantra abides, or the spot where it is in someone's hand, will become like a stūpa."

Then the youthful Mañjuśrī said this to the bodhisattva Vajrapāṇi: "Great bodhisattva, whoever holds, reads and recites these dhāraṇī-mantras, I will make offerings to him and serve him with reverence. I will always follow after him as well. When the time of his death comes, I will be his spiritual friend then too, and I will always show him the path to awakening."

"Mañjuśrī, excellent, excellent! These words are well spoken. Mañjuśrī, I too will make offerings to him. I will always follow after him as well. I will always protect him too. At the time of his death, too, I will give him a prophecy."

Then the youthful Mañjuśrī said this to the bodhisattva Vajrapāṇi:

"Great bodhisattva, explain in detail the analysis of the stages for accomplishing the siddhis of those dhāraṇī-mantras: by accomplishing which the bodhisattva great beings who practice the conduct of a bodhisattva through the gateway of secret mantra will be born into the family of the tathāgatas, and by accomplishing which dhāraṇī-mantras the mantra practitioner will obtain in this very life whatever accomplishment his mind desires."

When he had said this, the bodhisattva Vajrapāṇi said this to the youthful Mañjuśrī: "Mañjuśrī, listen, and I shall explain the practice of these dhāraṇī-mantras just as the buddhas have taught it. There is no need to match it to the lunar date or the constellation, and no need to fast. Nor will obstructors arise, for at the time of practicing the dhāraṇī-mantras one will always be blessed by all the buddhas of great miraculous power.

"Mañjuśrī, first of all, here is the rite of the painted image for these dhāraṇī-mantras. One should buy cloth at the price asked and see that it is free of hairs and the like. Then one should wash it with the five products of the cow and anoint it with scented water. Then, on an auspicious lunar date, at midnight and at a good moment, the painting should be made: one should have a painter who keeps the poṣadha vow draw it. That painter, too, should be protected with the Cakravartin. Every day one should also present food offerings for the deities and other offerings.

"Then in the center the painter should draw the Blessed Lady Prajñāpāramitā, seated cross-legged, with her hair in a topknot, absorbed in samādhi. To the left of the Blessed Lady he should draw Mahāpratisarā, seated upon a great king of utpalas, absorbed in samādhi, with her braided locks hanging down. On the right side is Vajrājitā, seated upon a white lotus, her body adorned with ornaments of every kind, holding a wheel in her left hand, her right hand paying homage to the Blessed Lady. In the upper part, too, he should draw two vidyādharas sending down a rain of flowers. In the lower part should be the practitioner, holding an incense bowl in hand, gazing at the Blessed Lady, holding a flowering branch, and seated cross-legged.

"Then the bodhisattva who practices the conduct of a bodhisattva through the gateway of secret mantra should stay in a clean place, a solitary place, on a mountain, in a forest, in a monastery, or wherever is agreeable to his mind. Eating alms food and keeping inner purity, before that painted image he should take up any one of the dhāraṇī-mantras and perform four hundred thousand recitations. At the end, on the day of the full moon, he should make offerings as best he can afford, and if he recites throughout the whole night, then in the morning, at sunrise, signs will appear. Rays of light will appear on the body of the mantra practitioner, and a rain of flowers will fall upon the image. The vidyādharas will raise a clamor. Thunder will roar. And the Blessed Lady will speak these words: 'Child, I have been accomplished; receive it!'

"Then the vidyādhara should present an offering of water and say this to the Blessed Lady: 'Fulfill my wishes!' Then the Blessed Lady takes the vidyādhara by the hand and places him in the maṇḍala of the assembly of all the tathāgatas. Through the power of the Blessed Lady, in an instant, a moment, a second, he accomplishes there the offering to all the tathāgatas, and in each instant he brings joy to all the realms of sentient beings. He will abide with many attendant vidyādharas in the maṇḍala of the assembly of all the tathāgatas.

"Mañjuśrī, this sūtra of the extensive rite of the painted image of the Blessed Lady accomplishes every aim, utterly removes all misdeeds, utterly purifies all fears, and utterly pacifies every untimely death. Mañjuśrī, whoever sees this painted image and makes offerings to it, in whose hand it is, and whoever writes it out in letters, to him the bodhisattvas will show the path to awakening, and he will become free of all misdeeds. He will be born from the merit of all the tathāgatas. He will become free of all fears. He will not be harmed by the evil māras. At the time of death he will see the Buddha."

"Vajrapāṇi, so it is. It is just as you have said. Great bodhisattva, whatever son of good family or daughter of good family sees this painted image, paints it, or has it in hand will be endowed with immeasurable qualities. He will be certain of unsurpassed, perfect and complete awakening, and he will not go to the lower realms and the bad destinies. He will always be born into the family of the tathāgatas, and all his aspirations will be completely fulfilled."

Then the youthful Mañjuśrī made this request to the bodhisattva Vajrapāṇi: "Great bodhisattva, tell the analysis of the stages for accomplishing the general siddhis of these dhāraṇī-mantras, by which those who practice the conduct of awakening through the gateway of secret mantra will, with little trouble and little time, please the buddhas and bodhisattvas, will perform miraculous displays of many kinds, and in the maṇḍala of the assembly of all the tathāgatas will free all sentient beings from all suffering."

When he had said this, the bodhisattva Vajrapāṇi said this to the youthful Mañjuśrī: "Mañjuśrī, listen, and in order to benefit sentient beings I shall explain the analysis of the general practice of these dhāraṇī-mantras. Mañjuśrī, any bodhisattva at all who practices the conduct of awakening through the gateway of secret mantra should set up the painted image and contemplate the Blessed Lady in his mind as form, as color, as shape or as letters. If he meditates in this way for six months, he will become capable of every rite. Poison will flee at his mere glance. When someone is seized by a graha, it will flee at a mere threat. Whatever else has seized someone will also let go at his mere glance. Even while staying beyond a hundred yojanas, he performs every rite. Against all fears, protection is made with a thread over which the mantra has been recited one hundred and eight times.

Against all obstructors, protection is made with water. Practitioners, or those who practice the rites of vetālas, should with any one of these secret mantras protect themselves, protect others, bind the directions, and seal the boundary of the maṇḍala.

Then, if one wishes to make the body invisible, under the constellation Puṣya one should buy realgar at the price asked and steep it in one's own blood. Then one should take it out and place it before the painted image. Then, eating a gruel of milk and flour as one's food, for seven days one should recite the mantra one hundred and eight times at each of the three times of day. Then, on the eighth day of the waning fortnight, without eating any food, one should place it in the left hand and recite the mantra of the Blessed Lady, and it will be accomplished. When a tilaka is made with it, the body becomes invisible, and one will have a form that changes at will, lasting five thousand years.

If one wishes to see the Blessed Lady, one should eat alms food for one month, go to a mountain peak, and recite the mantra of the Blessed Lady. Then one should fast for three days, and on the day of the full moon, at the foot of a bodhi tree, present food offerings for the deities as best one can afford, make offerings, and recite the mantra of the Blessed Lady. Then at midnight the Blessed Lady herself will come, and one should present her with an offering of water together with white flowers. Then the Blessed Lady strokes the crown of the practitioner's head and says, "Child, excellent! What boon shall I grant you?" When she says this, one should ask for whatever one wishes. If one wishes, one should ask for a mount, the state of a vidyādhara, invisibility, going beneath the earth, or anything else at all that one's mind desires; all of that one should ask for.

If one wishes to accomplish a haṃsa, one should make the figure of a haṃsa out of red lead and place it before the painted image. One should make an extensive offering with kunda flowers, and if one recites the mantra of the Blessed Lady throughout the whole night, then in the morning, at sunrise, it will be accomplished. Mounting it, one rises into the sky and becomes a second Brahmā.

If one wishes to accomplish a peacock, one should make the figure of a peacock out of clay and place it before the painted image. After reciting the mantra of the Blessed Lady for six months, at the end, on the day of the full moon, one should make offerings as best one can afford, and if one recites throughout the whole night, then at sunrise it will utter the cry of a peacock. As soon as the cry has sounded, the mantra practitioner should take it up. By merely taking it up, the mantra practitioner becomes like the son of the Pleiades (Kārttikeya). If, by the same rite, one accomplishes the son of the earth-protector (Airāvata), one becomes like Śakra (Indra).

If one wishes to accomplish invisibility, one should take a human skull and grind it finely together with licorice. Finally, on the eighth day of the waxing fortnight, one should kindle a fire of arka wood, present extensive offerings to the painted image, and perform a fire offering throughout the whole night. In the morning, if one marks the fingernails with its ash, the body becomes invisible, and one becomes the king of all who are invisible.

There is also another application. One should cook red lead together with butter and then place it in a copper vessel. Having made extensive offerings to the painted image, one should recite throughout the whole night until it blazes like crystal. Then it will be accomplished. If a tilaka is made with it, the body becomes invisible, lasting five thousand years.

If one wishes to accomplish an eye ointment, one should take jasmine flowers and ligbu mik and grind them finely. Then, as before, one should place them in a copper vessel, make offerings, and recite for one month. Then, when it has turned blue, if one anoints the eyes with it, whatever direction one looks toward will come under one's power. If one anoints the eyes with it, the king, the ministers, the chief queen, the princesses, and anyone else at all whom one looks at will come under one's power.

Then, if one wishes to accomplish the supreme eye ointment, on the eighth day of the waning fortnight one should take a corpse without wounds and present food offerings for the deities together with red sandalwood. Then one should place the eye ointment in the corpse's hand and recite. When one has recited, the eye ointment lying in the corpse's hand will move. Then it will be accomplished. If one anoints the eyes with it, whoever one looks at will come under one's power.

If one performs a fire offering with eight thousand jasmine flowers, all rites will be accomplished with ease.

If one enters a river that flows down to the ocean, beginning on the eighth day of the waning fortnight, and, eating alms food, recites the mantra a hundred thousand times, then on the eighth day of the waxing fortnight one will be granted a realm.

If on the eighth day of the waxing fortnight, eating alms food, one recites the mantra one thousand and eight times before the painted image, enemy armies will be completely pacified. Then, if, drinking milk as one's food, one recites it a hundred thousand times, the enemy army will be scattered.

If on the eighth day of the waning fortnight one fasts for one day, makes an extensive offering before the painted image, and recites throughout the whole night, then rays of light will come forth from the painted image. Then it will be accomplished: rajyaṃ dadāti, and it lasts for five hundred years.

If one enters a river that flows down to the ocean, recites the mantra a hundred thousand times, and drinks only water as one's food, then a sword will appear, held in the sky. One should present an offering of water and take it. By merely taking it, one's hair coils into locks, one comes to look like a youth of sixteen, and one goes about surrounded by many vidyādharas, with music played on many cymbals and drums. Every day one will see many buddha fields. Everywhere, too, one brings about clouds of offerings of every kind. And when one dies, one will be born in the pure buddha fields and born into the family of all the tathāgatas.

If one wishes to accomplish a sword, one should go to a solitary place and, drinking only water, recite the mantra for one month. After that, on the day of the full moon, one should make an extensive offering before the painted image as best one can afford, and if one recites throughout the whole night, then the sword will blaze. By merely taking it up, one becomes a vidyādhara, and one becomes the king of all vidyādharas.

…one will live for sixty thousand years. One will also make offerings, and one accomplishes every need just as one wishes. When one dies, one gains mastery of the first ground.

If one wishes food to arise inexhaustibly, one should take the root of the spoma plant and make a fire offering of it together with guḍūcī; then one's food will be inexhaustible.

If the eyes are diseased, one should enchant ground rock salt seven times and cover the eyes with it; the eye disease will then be cured.

If one grinds the root of the kalapiṅka with water free of living creatures and smears it around the navel, a woman will give birth with ease.

When the moon is seized by the wolf, one should put the butter of a tawny cow into a copper vessel and recite until it sizzles with a sound of "tsiriri." If one then drinks it, one will retain whatever one hears.

Having kept the fast of the poṣadha, beginning from the sixteenth day, if one makes a fire offering of sesame mixed with honey, rājyaṃ pratilabhyate. If one recites ten million times before the painted image, one will see the Blessed Lady Prajñāpāramitā, and she will teach one the Dharma. By hearing it, one will also become irreversible.

If one makes a vajra of red sandalwood, sets it at one's side, and recites until it blazes, then merely by touching it one becomes king of the four continents.

If one makes a fire offering of the fruits of the paṭa tree, whoever's name one pronounces will come under one's control. Juniper berries are the same: whoever's name one pronounces while making the fire offering will come under one's control. Or else, if one makes a fire offering of nimba fruits, whoever's name one pronounces will come under one's control.

If one makes a fire offering of bibhītaka flowers, one will obtain a great territory. If one makes a fire offering of mallikā flowers, one's food will never run out. If one makes a fire offering of a hundred thousand karavīra flowers and fruits, one will obtain a hundred thousand dīnāras. If one makes a fire offering of ten thousand jātī flowers, one will become the foremost royal chaplain. One should place the juice of brama together with honey in a copper vessel and recite until one obtains a dīnāra. By a fire offering of guggulu one will obtain a minor kingdom.

If one kindles a fire of bilva wood and makes a fire offering of a hundred thousand mallikā flowers, all the nāgas will come under one's control. If one kindles a fire of bibhītaka wood and makes a fire offering of ten thousand kindling sticks of aloeswood, all the yakṣas will come under one's control.

If one goes to a shrine of the Great God (Śiva), makes a maṇḍala with cow dung that has not fallen on the ground, and offers one thousand and eight arka flowers, one will see the Great God, and he will grant whatever accomplishment one desires. If one draws the painted image at a shrine of the Great God and recites a hundred thousand times, one will see the Husband of Umā, and he will grant whatever accomplishment one desires.

If one draws the painted image in a great charnel ground and recites one hundred and eight times, one will see a great vetāla. Even if he appears baring hideous fangs, like a great mass of fire, one should not be afraid of him; binding the mudrā, one should strike him, and he will fall down. When one then asks him for a boon, whether a kingdom, a treasure, invisibility, a land, or the power of travel on foot, he will grant any one of these five boons.

By a fire offering of salt one summons the women of the gods. If one makes a fire offering of one thousand and eight unopened jātī flowers, one will obtain one dīnāra every day. If one wishes an enemy to be deprived of life, one should make a fire offering of a hundred thousand oblations of salt, and he will be deprived of life.

If one wishes to accomplish a garuḍa, one should make the form of a garuḍa out of anthill clay and smear it with the five products of the cow. Then one should place that form of the garuḍa before the painted image and recite a hundred thousand times. It will then be accomplished, and mounting it, one goes wherever one wishes. It also procures all one's requisites. One will be free of all fears, and all the nāgas will come under one's control.

If one wishes to accomplish the Cakravartin, one should make a wheel of iron, wash it with scented water, and anoint it with saffron. Then one should place it on aśvattha leaves, set it out before the painted image, and recite three hundred thousand times. It will then be accomplished, and merely by holding it, one becomes a vidyādhara cakravartin, the king of all vidyādharas. Surrounded by many koṭis of vidyādharas and surrounded by many koṭis of divine maidens, one travels through the sky within a divine palace and constantly makes offerings.

If one wishes to accomplish the Blessed Lady, one should recite six hundred thousand times before the painted image. One will then see the Blessed Lady directly, and merely by seeing her one will follow in the conduct of the tathāgatas. One will also attain meditative absorption, and one will gain the companionship of the bodhisattvas.

If one wishes to pacify, one should offer one thousand and eight unopened lotuses, and the one for whose sake they were offered will be pacified. If one makes a fire offering of one thousand and eight jātī flowers, all of that person's illnesses will vanish. If one makes a fire offering of one thousand and eight bibhītaka flowers, there will be no untimely death, and one will be embraced by good fortune. If one makes a fire offering of ten thousand unopened atimuktaka flowers, then whatever graha has seized the person for whose sake it is done will release him at merely a glance.

When the moon is seized by the wolf, one should place butter in a copper vessel and recite until smoke billows from it. If one then drinks it, one will retain whatever one hears.

If one wishes to accomplish a peacock, one should take earth from both banks of a river, mix it with sandalwood, and make the form of a peacock. Then one should place that peacock before the painted image and recite until it utters the cry of a peacock; it will then become an accomplishment.

If one wishes to accomplish wealth, one should offer a hundred thousand karavīra flowers, and one will obtain five dīnāras every day. If one enters water up to the throat and recites a hundred thousand times, one will find a treasure. If one makes a fire offering of a hundred thousand unopened jātī flowers, one will obtain whatever woman one desires.

If one offers ten thousand atimuktaka flowers, whatever illnesses are in the body will all be swept away, and they will not arise again. One will also become long-lived, good fortune will arise in one's body, and one will live for a hundred years.

If one wishes to destroy a hostile army, one should enchant one's finger seven times and threaten with it, and the army will flee. If one wishes them to fight, one should make a fire offering of a hundred thousand owl feathers, and they will fight. If one wishes to ruin an enemy, one should make a fire offering of a hundred thousand mustard seeds, and the enemy will be deprived of life. If one makes a fire offering of ten thousand juniper berries dipped in ghee with the three hot spices, no obstructors will harm one. If one makes a fire offering of one thousand and eight bibhītaka fruits dipped in white mustard oil, the enemy will be ruined within five days.

For bringing under control, one should make a figure out of śāli rice flour, cut it into pieces, and make a fire offering of it; within seven days even Śakra will come under one's control. If one makes a fire offering of unopened karavīra flowers, the yakṣiṇīs will come under one's control. If one makes a fire offering of jātī leaves, one drives mad whomever one wishes. jatikalikanan lakṣaṃ juhuyad rājānaṃ baśikaroti. If one makes a fire offering of one thousand and eight atimuktaka flowers, the land will come under one's control. By a fire offering of ghee, quartan fever will vanish. If one makes a fire offering of jātī flowers mixed with honey, one will obtain one dīnāra every day.

Next, one who wishes to accomplish the nāgas should make a fire offering of a hundred thousand unopened mallikā flowers. All the nāgas will then be accomplished, and all of them there are will accomplish whatever one wishes. If one makes a fire offering of ten thousand unopened aśoka flowers, all the yakṣiṇīs will be accomplished, and every day they will present food and clothing for five hundred servants. If one wishes to bring the yakṣiṇīs under control, one should make a fire offering of a hundred thousand kindling sticks of aloeswood; all the yakṣiṇīs will then come under one's control and will accomplish whatever one wishes.

If one wishes to accomplish Vajrapāṇi, one should recite four hundred thousand times before the painted image. Finally, on the full moon, one should make offerings of utpala flowers as best one can afford and recite through the whole night. Then in the morning, at sunrise, Vajrapāṇi will come, and having come he will touch the head of the vidyādhara. Merely by that touch, one becomes endowed with the five superknowledges. One will obtain the state of a vidyādhara itself, or anything else whatsoever, and by this one will obtain all that one wishes.

If one offers a hundred thousand kiṃśuka flowers before the image of Vajrapāṇi, one will obtain a thousand dīnāras. If one takes hold of the hand of the image of Vajrapāṇi and recites for one month, it will give one dīnāra every day. If one places the image of Vajrapāṇi on lotus petals and offers ten thousand unopened jātī flowers, it will give one kārṣāpaṇa every day.

If for one month one rises at midnight, makes a maṇḍala with gorocanā every day, and recites the mantra of the Blessed Lady, one will become able to perform every rite, and obstructors will not harm one. If for one month one smears the maṇḍala with red sandalwood and recites the mantra of the Blessed Lady, all the piśācas will come under one's control and will do whatever one wishes. If one smears the maṇḍala with one's own blood and recites for one month, all the ḍākinīs will come under one's control and will do whatever one wishes.

If one makes a fire offering of a hundred thousand pieces of white calamus, all the mātṛkās will be accomplished and will accomplish whatever one wishes. If one makes a fire offering of a thousand ligadur seeds, one will obtain a full vessel every day. If one makes a fire offering of the root of the geushar plant, one will obtain four kārṣāpaṇas every day. If one makes a fire offering of ten thousand apāmārga kernels, inexhaustible wealth will arise. If one makes a fire offering of one thousand and eight peppercorns, takes the ash, makes a mark with it, and scatters it into heaps of peas, lentils, and the like, they will become inexhaustible.

If one wishes to accomplish Mañjuśrī, one should smear a maṇḍala with gorocanā mixed with aloeswood, place the image there, and recite for one month. One will then see Mañjuśrī himself, and he will teach one the Dharma. On seeing him, one will retain whatever one hears, and one will also attain the state of a vidyādhara; he fulfills it for as long as one wishes. If one offers one thousand and eight white lotuses, Mañjuśrī will be accomplished for one, and he will grant any one of five boons: kingship, entry into the underworld, invisibility, retention of what one hears, or rejuvenation through elixirs.

If one makes a fire offering of one thousand and eight kindling sticks of āmalaka, all the yakṣiṇīs there are will come under one's control and will accomplish whatever one wishes. If one makes a fire offering of a hundred thousand bilva fruits, all the Śrīs there are will be accomplished and will accomplish whatever one wishes. najyadima prayadcanti. If one makes a fire offering of a hundred thousand bibhītaka seeds, all the Sarasvatīs will be accomplished, and through their accomplishment one's speech will become unimpeded. If one makes a fire offering of ten thousand unopened priyaṅgu flowers, one will obtain five kārṣāpaṇas every day.

If one wishes to put everyone to sleep, one should draw a painted image of the god who is lord of the city, make a fire offering of mustard seed, carry its ash into the city, and scatter it. They will all fall asleep and remain so, and one may do whatever one wishes. Just as for a city, one should do likewise for a country.

One who wishes to dry up a pond should make a fire offering of ten thousand oblations of mustard seed smeared with one's own blood, take its ash, and scatter it in that pond. The pond will dry up, and the nāgas who dwell there will present jewels to one, just as one wishes.

If one makes a fire offering of a hundred thousand kindling sticks of apāmārga, one will see the goddess Śrī. sa ca rājyama prayacchati. If one makes a fire offering of one thousand and eight bilva fruits, one will become the royal chaplain of a great king. If one makes a fire offering of a hundred thousand kindling sticks of aśoka, all four-footed creatures will come under one's control. If one makes a fire offering of a hundred thousand jātī flowers, one will become worthy to have one's words heeded in the circle of the royal palace. If one makes a fire offering of a hundred thousand jātī flowers, all women will be brought under one's control. If one makes a fire offering of a hundred kindling sticks from the mustard plant dipped in honey, all the grahas will become peaceful toward one.

If one scatters rice porridge and honey into flowing water, all the nāginīs will come under one's control, and they will even lead one to their own abodes. If one wishes to accomplish the vināyakas, one should scatter a hundred thousand atimuktaka flowers, and all the vināyakas will grant accomplishments. If one makes a fire offering of one thousand and eight aśvattha leaves dipped in honey, all the yakṣiṇīs will present that vidyādhara every day with a full vessel of whatever he wishes. If one makes a fire offering of one thousand and eight peacock feathers, all the harms of Māra will be dispelled.

With aloeswood: mardanaṃ astahasasran juhuyāda rāja baśo bhavati. With kindling sticks of priyaṅgu: mahārāja gramahiśi baśo bhavati.

If one kindles a fire of aloeswood kindling sticks and makes a fire offering of ten thousand sesame seeds, the land will come under one's control. If one kindles a fire of milky-sapped wood and makes a fire offering of a hundred thousand sesame seeds, all the piśācas will come under one's control and will do whatever one wishes. If one kindles a fire of madaka wood and makes a fire offering of one thousand and eight peppercorns, all the bhūtas will come under one's control, and each of the lords of the bhūtas will also give food and clothing for a hundred servants every day. Wherever one wishes to go, one goes there riding on their shoulders. If one makes a fire offering of a hundred thousand peas, all the bhūtas will come under one's control, and they will procure all the requisites of that vidyādhara.

Mañjuśrī, whatever rite one applies within the practices of these dhāraṇī-mantras, beginning with these, it grants the accomplishment of that rite. Whether the aims and the rites differ, and whether the rites are many or few, it accomplishes them. Furthermore, Mañjuśrī, obstructors have no opportunity against those who hold and practice these secret mantras.

From the Vajrapāṇi Empowerment, the ritual for accomplishing the attainments of the mothers of all the tathāgatas is complete.

The great king of tantras, The Noble Empowerment of Vajrapāṇi, is complete.

This was translated, edited, and finalized by the Indian preceptor Śīlendrabodhi and the chief editor-translator, Bandé Yeshé Dé.

This concludes the translation. The following are translator's notes.

"Vaśavartin" renders ལྷའི་རྒྱལ་པོ་དབང་སྒྱུར, the lord of the Paranirmitavaśavartin heaven. "Great jewels, lords of power" renders ནོར་བུ་ཆེན་པོ་དབང་གི་རྒྱལ་པོ, which may denote a specific gem (possibly sapphire, indranīla). "Throat" renders མགུར, which here appears to be the bodily point from which the rays issue and to which they return. ཡ་མཚན་གྱི་ས་སྙིང is read as the evident ཡ་མཚན་གྱི་སྙིང, "hearts full of wonder," matching the preceding sentence.  "Linkages" renders མཚམས་སྦྱོར་བ, the junctions by which world systems join into ever larger groupings in this cosmology. In the last sentence the phrase "heart-essence of the letters" is written twice in succession (ཡི་གེའི་སྙིང་པོའི་ཡི་གེའི་སྙིང་པོས); this looks like a copyist's repetition and is rendered once.  The names of the northeastern buddha (རིན་པོ་ཆེ་མཆོག་འཛིན) and bodhisattva (རིན་པོ་ཆེ་ཅོད་པན) could not be identified with confidence and are translated by meaning. "Intimations of his own body" renders ལུས་ཀྱི་རྣམ་པར་རིག་བྱེད (kāyavijñapti), bodily expression. སྙིང་པོ་དའི is read as the evident སྙིང་པོ་འདི, "this heart-essence."  Several names of buddhas and bodhisattvas here could not be identified with confidence and are translated by meaning: Thunder King (འབྲུག་སྒྲའི་རྒྱལ་པོ), Essence of the Pure Jewel Lotus (རིན་པོ་ཆེའི་པདྨ་རྣམ་པར་དག་པའི་སྙིང་པོ) and Lotus Limb (པདྨའི་ཡན་ལག). The southwestern buddha is called Mahāvajraketu and then Vajraketu, and the northwestern buddha is called Sughoṣa and then "most melodious of voice"; these appear to be variant forms of the same names.  The name of the bodhisattva of the zenith (གཡུལ་ལས་ཤིན་ཏུ་རྣམ་པར་རྒྱལ་བ, then shortened to གཡུལ་ལས་རྣམ་པར་རྒྱལ་བ) could not be identified with confidence and is translated by meaning. In the verse "Just so his speech, just so those beings," the word "beings" (སེམས་ཅན) is unexpected and may be a slip; it is translated as written.  The text reads བསྟན་པ་ནས་པར, which makes no sense; it is taken as a slip for བསྟན་པ་གནས་པར, "so that the teaching would endure." The phrase "all the dharmas of the world systems of the great trichiliocosm" (ཆོས་ཐམས་ཅད) is rendered loosely as "all that belongs to" those world systems.  Several names in the lists could not be identified with confidence and are translated literally: among the monks, Great Power, Great Tooth, Kāśyapa Son of the Lord of Conduct, and Close Union; among the bodhisattvas, several descriptive names; among the asura lords, Deeply Content, Supreme Field, Great Drum, Crest, Moon Face, Destroyer, Profound One, Proud Crest, and Garlanded with Variety. Baṭi is given as the Tibetan transcribes it. The asura Virocana (rnam par snang ba) is distinct from the buddha Vairocana. The city name Glorious Source of Qualities is also given literally.  Many of the names in these lists could not be identified with confidence and are translated literally, among them several nāga kings (Hundred Rays, Lightning Tongue, Without Chaff, Moving by Strength, Cropped Ear, Lotus Bearer, One Head, Deceiver, Voice of Indra, Mighty One), the yakṣa generals Lord of Waters Who Holds the Confounder and Hundred Hands, the garuḍa lords other than Vainateya, the rākṣasa lords, the winds, and the solar deities. The great sage "Fire" (me) is probably Agni. Dragpo, "the Fierce One," at the head of the hungry ghosts is taken as Rudra. The rākṣasa leaders are called "lords of rākṣasīs" in the Tibetan, though their retinue is of rākṣasas.  "Son of the Pleiades" (smin drug gi bu) is the usual epithet of Kārttikeya; its use here for a "king of the planets" is unusual, so the epithet is kept literal. The seven wives of Rudra, Brahmā, Viṣṇu, Kumāra, Kubera, the Boar and the Great Terrifier correspond to the mother goddesses (Raudrī, Brāhmī, Vaiṣṇavī, Kaumārī, Kauberī, Vārāhī, and probably Bhairavī). Many other names (several cloud gods, lightnings, mountains, river goddesses, gandharva, vidyādhara and bhūta kings, and most of the rākṣasīs) could not be identified with confidence and are translated literally. "Lake-Reliant" may be the Sarayū. In the vidyādhara list, གཞན་ང is read as གཞན་ཡང, "and others too."  "Vitality-stealers," "oppressors," and "shadow-oppressors" render classes of spirits (mdangs 'phrog pa, gnon po, grib gnon) whose Sanskrit equivalents are not certain. The gem spug is rendered musāragalva by convention.  The text says Samantabhadra "gave voice to this inspired utterance" but no utterance follows before he falls silent; this is translated as it stands. In "made a sign to come," the Tibetan reads འོད་བར་འགྱུར་བ, evidently a slip for འོང་བར་འགྱུར་བ ("to come"), which the context requires. འབྲོག་གནས is taken as the standard rendering of the yakṣa general Āṭavaka. ནཱ་ད་ཀུ་བེ་ར is kept as spelled; it presumably corresponds to Nalakūbara (Naḍakūbara), Kubera's son. "With the ears of an elephant" (གླང་པོ་རྣ་བ) may be an epithet or a yakṣa name (Gajakarṇa); it is translated literally.  "Rows like doves" (ཕུག་རོན་ལྟ་བུའི་ཕྲེང) probably refers to dove-shaped cornice ornaments (Skt. kapotapālī); translated literally. ཕེབ་རྡོ, rendered "chime-stones," is an instrument not securely identified. ས་བརྟོལ ("earth-piercing") is a divine flower whose Sanskrit name could not be identified with confidence and is translated literally.  The opening sentence is taken as the close of the Buddha's preceding reply, foretelling Samantabhadra's question. གང་ཞེན is read as གང་ཞེ་ན ("what is it?"), which the context requires; the phrase "embraced by deeds and actions" appears twice in the Tibetan, apparently by repetition, and is given once. "It is good that you ask" renders ཡང་ལགས་སོ, read in the sense of approval.  In the list of what is absent from the nature of reality, the phrase "neither training" occurs twice in the Tibetan, evidently by repetition, and is given once. The first ཟླ་བ in the list of natural phenomena is taken as "moon" and the last, after "yearly cycle," as "month."  སློད་ནས, describing how Śākyamuni and the gathered tathāgatas spoke the mantra, is unclear; it is rendered "together with" from context. The last verse line, ཀུན་གྱི་དབང་ནི་བདག་ལགས་སོ, may also mean "I am the one for the empowerment of all."  The architectural term rendered "with a plinth" is uncertain; it is taken as a variant of the term for the ledge running around a maṇḍala palace wall. The lines "You who have proclaimed the king of secret mantra, why do you now remain without speaking?" could also be read as addressing the Buddha as "king of secret mantra."  The rare term rendered "fixing the earth" and "the Vajra that Fixes the Earth" is taken as referring to the vajra peg that stakes down the ground, as named in the heading of that mantra. The phrase rendered "whether they were awake" reads literally "not lying down."  Two classes of spirit could not be identified with a standard Sanskrit name and are translated by meaning: the "oppressor spirits" and the "shadow oppressors." The spirits "that cause loss of memory" are taken as the apasmāras, the standard Sanskrit equivalent of the Tibetan term.  "The three dharmas" through whose blessing Vajrapāṇi is blessed are not specified in this passage. The opening of Vajrapāṇi's reply is read as "Lord of the Dharma" addressed to the Buddha.  "Ancestral spirits" renders a term read as the spirits of the paternal ancestors; "siddhas" and "serpent seizers" in the same list are classes of graha translated as they stand. "Words that need no seeking" renders a literal phrase whose sense seems to be plain, unmistakable speech.  "Mixed" reads འདྲེས་མ for the written འདྲེན་མ, which makes no sense in a list of soil colors. "Śrāvaka Vehicle" reads ཐེག་པ for the written ཐག་པ ("rope"). "Honor them" reads མཆོད for the written མ་ཆོད. "The vehicle of great beings" takes སྐྱེད་ཆེན as སྐྱེས་ཆེན; the reading is uncertain. "Human flesh" renders ཤ་ཆེན, literally "great flesh," the standard tantric term. "The wise Vajra, the bestower of the supreme" (mchog sbyin rdo rje blo ldan) appears to be an epithet of the tantra's presiding deity, presumably Vajrapāṇi.  "That called Vajra Invincible Fire" and "the Queen of Knowledge Mantras, the Fearless One" translate names I cannot identify with confidence. "It is the mother of all wise bodhisattvas" reads ma yin te as "is the mother"; the words could also mean "is not of all," but that makes poor sense here. The bird called bzhad, read here with the preceding word as "the cry of vultures," and the "white birds" are uncertain identifications. "Dhanada" (ནོར་སྦྱིན) is normally another name of Kubera, who is also listed. The text gives both.  Among the four world protectors, Kubera (ལུས་ངན) stands where Dhṛtarāṣṭra, guardian of the east, would be expected, since Vaiśravaṇa is listed separately. The translation follows the text. "Mixed" for the remaining petals reads འདྲེས་མ for the written འདྲེན་མ, as in the preceding piece. The tathāgatas of the four intermediate directions are identified by the standard Tibetan renderings of their names: gtsug tor can as Śikhin, rnam par gzigs as Vipaśyin, thams cad skyob as Viśvabhū and 'khor ba 'jig as Krakucchanda. "Iron vajra" (lcags kyi rdo rje) may be a slip for lcags kyu, "hook."  "The carelessly held vajra" renders rdo rje bab col, and its exact sense is uncertain. Some names could not be identified with confidence and are translated by meaning: "the Averter of Austerities" (dka' thub zlog pa) and the gandharva king "Possessor of Many-Colored Garlands" (sna tshogs phreng ba can). "Cymbals" for pheg is a tentative rendering. The text speaks of "four sisters" but gives mudrās for Tumburu, their brother, as well as for the four.  The lists of woods, incense, flowers and foods contain many words that Tibetan transcribes from Indian terms, and I cannot identify some of them with confidence. These are given as transcribed (salakrī, cepok, kyanar, dongka, dhanukari, aśovarti, venaka, śirebhedaka, garbhodakaraka, berek, dvetse, drengbu, shunthapālikā, zhuteu, sgeu, bhegana, saṃbhadhi, khari). The four texts read are identified by their Tibetan titles: the Prajñāpāramitā, the Suvarṇaprabhāsottama, the Tathāgataguhya and the Gaṇḍavyūha ("Array of the Stem"). "Protectors" reads skyob pa for the written sdzob pa. The text gives twenty-five vases but accounts explicitly only for eight, a ninth and a tenth.  The line "and I as well, are lesser than his heart" renders the Tibetan literally; the sense appears to be that where a bodhisattva master dwells, even the bodhisattvas and Vajrapāṇi himself are of less account than him, but the exact force of "heart" is uncertain. "The offering fee is drawn forth" renders ཡོན་དགུག literally; it refers to the dakṣiṇā due to the master.  "Amṛtakuṇḍalin" renders བདུད་རྩི་ཐབ་སྦྱོར, taken as a variant name for the wrathful guardian usually called བདུད་རྩི་འཁྱིལ་བ; a literal reading would be "a nectar hearth arrangement." "Ratnakusuma" and "Saṃkusumita" render རིན་པོ་ཆེ་མེ་ཏོག and མེ་ཏོག་ཀུན་དུ་རྒྱས་པ, read as names of buddhas heading the list of eight blessing deities (one for each lotus petal); they could alternatively be read as "jewel flowers and fully blossomed flowers" among the contents of the vases.  In the first statement about the buddhas and bodhisattvas teaching result, attainment and purification, the Tibetan reads "abiding on the ground of karma and maturation," whereas every later parallel reads "the ground free of karma and maturation"; it is translated as written, though a negative may have dropped out.  In the passage on who casts aside this world, the first sentence reads "abides in the unconditioned element," which contradicts the following sentence; it is translated as "conditioned," taking the negative syllable as a copyist's slip. "A seal" renders རྒྱལ, read as རྒྱ་ལ ("relying on a seal"), which the following references to the seal's impression require. The final sentence is terse; "the other" may refer to the target of the rite.  "Vajra Upsurge" and "Upsurge" render ལྷངས, a word suggesting a sudden blazing or surging up; the intended Sanskrit name is uncertain. The opening sentence is elliptical: the being summoned and pressed down under the left foot is understood to be the target of the fierce rite, and "kindles the fire within oneself" renders བདག་ལ་མེ་སྦར་བས, which may instead mean simply that one kindles the fire oneself.  The name rendered "Vajra Great Terrifying One" (rdo rje 'jigs 'jigs lta chen po, literally "great one of terrifying appearance") could not be matched to a Sanskrit form with confidence and is translated by meaning. The same applies to "Great Vajra of the Stride" and "Vajra Peak." The wrathful one manifested as "Vajra Mahādaṇḍa" (great club bearer) is the figure whose mantra is labelled the mantra of Vajra Nīladaṇḍa (blue club bearer). In the list of Vajra Peak's six-armed attributes only five objects are named; the last, kha ba, is taken in its sense of "thorn," which is uncertain.  Several of the deities named only in these mantra colophons are given in English by meaning (Vajra Banner, Vajra Garland, Vajra Trident Bearer, Vajra Fang, Vajra Hammer, Vajra Fence, Vajra Dagger, Vajra Net, Vajra Laughter, Vajra Charm, Vajra Great Drum, Vajra Bell, Vajra Body Hair, Vajra Clawed One, Vajra Messenger, Vajra Crown, Vajra Red Body), since their Sanskrit names cannot be fixed with confidence; Vajrakulasundarī and Vajrāṅkuśī are supported by their mantras.  Most deities named only in these mantra colophons are given in English by meaning, since their Sanskrit names cannot be fixed with confidence. "Vajra Nadakubera" keeps the Tibetan transcription of the name (na da ku be ra), which probably stands for Nalakūbara or Naḍakūbara. The colophon rendered "Vajra Wind Blaze" (rlung tshig) and "Vajra Formation" ('chags pa) are uncertain in sense.  Most of these deities are named only in the mantra colophons and are given in English by meaning where no Sanskrit form can be fixed with confidence. "Vajra Plumed Lady" (sgros ma), "Vajra Taste" (rdo rjer ro ma) and "Vajra Great Time" (dus po che) are uncertain readings of obscure names. "Vajra Doe" (sha bkra mo) follows the mantra's haraṇa, apparently for hariṇī, "doe". "Attendant" renders phyag brnyan (Skt. kiṃkara), which the mantras themselves echo.  Many deities named only in the mantra colophons and in the list of youths are given in English by meaning where no Sanskrit form can be fixed with confidence; the Sanskrit is given where the mantra or a standard rendering supports it (Susiddhikara, Vidyottama, Gajakarṇa, Bhīṣaṇa, Subāhu, Vajrasena). The body part kos ko, rendered "chin," is uncertain. "Maidservant" renders bran mo.  The sentence introducing Māmakī's mantra is obscure: Vajrapāṇi utters the sound "mine" (Tibetan bdag gi, evidently Sanskrit mama, punning on the name Māmakī, "She Who Says Mine"), together with the phrase rendered "Let it never be done!", whose exact force is uncertain. Several of the youths' names (Vajra Army, Vajra Breast, Vajra Holder of the Supreme Ear, and the like) are rendered from their Tibetan meaning, in keeping with the style already used for this list.  "The plume of fire in the great island of the ocean" renders the Tibetan literally; it probably alludes to the submarine fire (vaḍavāgni) said to devour the ocean's waters. In the assembly's verse, "free of knots" renders a word (tshigs, "joints, knots") whose exact sense here is uncertain. The name of the queen of knowledge is rendered from its Tibetan meaning, as its Sanskrit form cannot be identified with confidence.  The text calls the Four Great Kings here "great generals of the yakṣas." Tibetan ཆུ་དབང་རྨུགས་བྱེད་འཛིན is taken as Jambhala Jalendra, "Jambhala, Lord of Waters," which the mantra's "jaṃbha jalodbhava" supports. འབྲོག་གནས is identified as the yakṣa general Āṭavaka.  "Eight Arms" (ལག་བརྒྱད་པ) and the gandharva king "Variegated Garland" (སྣ་ཚོགས་ཕྲེང་བ་ཅན) are rendered from their Tibetan meaning, as their Sanskrit names cannot be identified with confidence. "The youth" to whom offerings are made in Eight Arms' rite is unspecified in the text. "The gate of the Glorious Gate" (དཔལ་གྱི་སྒོ་མོའི་སྒོ) is rendered literally; it seems to name an entrance to the asura realm. The doubled word for "poison" (དུག་དུག) is rendered once.  The garuḍa king "Son of Māra," the rākṣasa king "Terrifying," and the wind god "Cloud Garland" are rendered from their Tibetan meaning, as their Sanskrit names cannot be identified with confidence. "Raising and stopping" the wind is inferred from the context of the wind gods' rite; the Tibetan says only "producing and stopping and so on."  "Moonstone" renders zla ba nyal shel, read as a variant of zla ba chu shel (candrakānta). In the Sun's rite, the verb bus with the five-wicked lamp is unclear (perhaps for byas, "make"); rendered "set up". The khri'i zur shing ("bed-corner post") is taken as the khaṭvāṅga. The preta king Drag po is rendered Rudra, given the Śaiva attributes (ash, trident, khaṭvāṅga, tiger skin) and his stated etymology "the Fierce One".  The goddess dKa' thub zlog ("She Who Averts Austerities") is taken as Umā, whose name is traditionally etymologized as "u mā," "do not [practice austerities]," and whose mantra here reads umaye. "The Auspicious One" renders bzang ldan, whose referent I could not identify with confidence; the phrase "la sa cho ga" is read as "las cho ga," "the rite from." The "great planet, son of the Pleiades" (smin drug gi bu) is taken as Kārttikeya (Skanda). The cloud god "White Cloud" and the four lightning deities are translated literally, as their Sanskrit names could not be identified with confidence. "Eye salve" renders lig bu mig (añjana); "bezoar" renders gi wang (gorocanā).  The names in the list of Yama's company are taken as Kālarātri (mtshan mo nag mo), Kāladaṇḍa (be con nag po can), Kālapuruṣa (skyes bu nag po) and Kālakarṇī (rna nag mo). dPal sbyin ma ("Glory-Giver") in the opening list and dPa' byed ("Hero-Maker"), who later speaks in her place, could not be identified with confidence and are translated literally; they may be variants of one name. 'Chi bdag is rendered Mṛtyu. In Yama's rite, bri bar bgyi'o ("should write") is read as dri bar bgyi'o, "should ask." The flower da tu ke ra ka is unidentified (possibly dhattūra or ketaka).  The goddesses are identified from their husbands: the consorts of Viṣṇu, Kumāra (Kaumārī, gzhon nu ma), the Boar (Vārāhī) and Kubera (lus ngan). The husband of the goddess whose mantra names Yamadūtī, 'jigs 'jigs lta chen po ("the Great Dreadful-Looking One"), could not be identified with confidence and is translated literally. "A place where merit flows down" (bsod nams 'bab pa) may mean a sacred bathing place (puṇyatīrtha). Srul po is taken as a pūtanā demoness.  Several names could not be identified with confidence and are translated literally: the vidyādhara king Tog ("Crest"), the rākṣasī So can ("the Toothed One"), the bhūta king brTsegs pa ("Heaped"), and the gnon po spirits ("oppressors") with their leader 'Gul po ("Shaker"). Ba lang skyong ba is taken as Gopāla. The bird bheruṇaṭika (perhaps the bhāruṇḍa) and the pati tree are unidentified. Gzegs ma, rendered "grit," is uncertain.  The names of the leaders of the shadow-oppressors (rnam par 'byam pa, rendered "the Sprawler") and of the young gods of Māra's class (legs rgyal, rendered "Fine Victor", possibly Sujaya) could not be identified with confidence and are translated by meaning. The one who comes with Rati and Prīti at midnight is presumably Kāmadeva, whose image is worshiped; Rati and Prīti render dga' ma and sdug ma, his two consorts. "The six-toned song" and "the melody of the hundred-feathered one" (glu drug ldan, 'dab brgya ma) are uncertain musical terms, possibly the ṣaḍja note and a bird's song. Jayā, Vijayā, Ajitā and Aparājitā are the four sisters associated with their brother Tumburu.  The sentence rendered "one should bind the Person just as it has been set out on oneself" is obscure; the written ད་གོད is read as དགོད, "set out", and the "Person" and the "Four-Faced One" (often an epithet of Brahmā) appear to name mudrās or arrangements of the essence. The substance rendered "collyrium" (lig bu mig) is uncertain.  "The Soaring Ones" and "the Soaring One" translate 'gro lding, a land and a vidyā king that could not be identified with confidence. "Banyan trees" reads shing pa ta as the vaṭa tree. In "the great yakṣas and Kubera", the written nad ("illness") before ku be ra is taken as a slip for dang ("and"). "Pits" for stor khung is uncertain.  The "seat" of the wheel renders mchod rten khri, read as a stūpa of the Sugata a span high serving as a throne or stand; the phrase is terse.  "The abode of the king of the Vaśavartin gods" renders rgyal po dbang sgyur gyi gnas, presumably the Paranirmitavaśavartin heaven. "Juniper berry" for rgya shug gi tshig gu is a probable reading.  "Not mixed with glue" reads སྦྱིན ("giving") as སྤྱིན ("glue, size"), which the context plainly requires; the instruction parallels the standard rule that image colors be free of hide glue. The place described as དུགས་མེད་པ is rendered "free of smoke"; the word is uncertain. The vidyādhara in the east is written ཡན་ལག་བཀྲས་བྱིན; it is taken as the Citrāṅga named earlier in the text. ཏོག་ལྡན, the vidyādhara of the west, could not be identified with confidence and is translated literally as "Crest-Bearer". "Śrī" (དཔལ) is most likely the goddess Śrī (Lakṣmī), to whom lotus fire offerings are typically directed. ལིག་བུ་མིག is rendered "collyrium" (añjana), as the third of the usual triad of bezoar, realgar, and collyrium. "Short garment" for གོས་ཚིག་གུ is tentative.  "Glue" again reads སྦྱིན as སྤྱིན ("size, glue"), as in the earlier instruction on paints. The plant ཏྣ་སུ་ཙི (tnasuci) could not be identified and is kept as transliterated. ཆེ་གེ་མོ is the Tibetan placeholder "such-and-such", so the mountain is left unnamed. Vajradhara Abiding Without Elaboration (རྡོ་རྗེ་འཆང་སྤྲོས་པ་མེད་པར་གནས་པ) is given in English, as no Sanskrit form could be identified with confidence. བློ་གྲོས་ལྡན is taken as Matimān.  The names of the two tathāgatas, Glory of Fragrant Light (དྲི་ཞིམ་སྣང་དཔལ) and Utterly Crushing with the Firm Vajra (རྡོ་རྗེ་སྲ་བས་རབ་ཏུ་འཇོམས་པ), could not be identified with confidence and are translated literally. The heart mantra is called འཇིག་པའི་མེ ("fire of destruction") in the prose and འཇིགས་པའི་མེ ("terrifying fire") in Mañjuśrī's answer. These are taken as the same name and rendered "Terrifying Fire" both times. In Śāntimati's question, འཇམ་དཔལ་གྱི is read as an address to Mañjuśrī.  "Perform the binding on a man" (སྐྱེས་བུ་བཅིང་བར་བྱས) is literal. It probably refers to tying a protective cord or amulet on the warrior, but the text does not specify. The wood called གྲུ་ཚལ (grutsal) could not be identified. In the rite of taking up the vidyā, འཇིགས་མེད is read adverbially ("fearlessly"), not as the name of the king of vidyās.  "When the moon is seized by the wolf" (ཟླ་བ་སྤྱང་ཀིས་ཟིན་པ) evidently refers to a lunar eclipse. ན་ད་ཀུ་བེ་ར, the son of the yakṣa lord Kubera, is identified as Nalakūbara. "A place with a single liṅga" renders མཚན་གཅིག་ཡོད་པ, taken as the familiar siddhi site of a solitary liṅga shrine (ekaliṅga). The same expression with མཚན་མ in the previous passage was rendered "a single landmark".  ཞི་བ་མོ ("peaceful") in "another's ཞི་བ་མོའི་ཆོ་ག" is read as ཞིབ་མོ, "detailed rite," which the same passage later spells out (ཞིབ་མོའི་ཆོ་ག). The phrase ལག་ན་རྡོ་རྗེ་དགའ་བ is rendered "beloved of Vajrapāṇi"; it might instead be part of the deity's name. ཐོད་ཟེའུ་ཀ is uncertain and is rendered "head-wrap". In "a sword shaped like a crow's beak", the Tibetan says literally a crow's "tooth". In the apāmārga rite, འཆོས་ནམ་བསླངས་པར is read as འཆོས་ན་མ་བསླངས་པར, "if one chews it, without begging".  In the servants' practice, སྒྲ་ཤིག་ཤིག་པོར་འདུག་པ is uncertain; it is rendered "staying amid a rattling noise". ལྕེ་འབབ་པས་བཅག་པ is taken as wood split by lightning.  "When the moon is seized by the wolf" translates the Tibetan literally; it evidently refers to a lunar eclipse, with the moon "released" when it ends. སྤང་མ is taken as spikenard, and རྒྱ་སྤོས is left untranslated (rgya spos) because it cannot be securely identified. In the verse, ཨ་ཤྭད་ལོ་མ་འབར་བཅུག is read as ...བར་བཅུག, "placed between aśvattha leaves". The lines ཆུ་འདུས་ག་ལ་བར and ཁོང་པ་ན་ང་གི་རྣམ་དག་པར are obscure; "at a confluence of waters" and "within a pure hollow" are tentative. "The two-mouthed nāga king" is a literal rendering; it probably names a kind of snake.  The line འཇིག་རྟེན་དྲུག་ཆ་ཕྲོགས་ནས་སུ is obscure and is translated literally ("a sixth part" may allude to the king's sixth share). ཀུརྱ་ཚང་མའི་མཇུག་མ is tentatively taken as the tail feathers of a bird called kurya. ལྷང་ཚེར and དྲི་སན་ཏ could not be identified and are left as "lhang tsher" and "drisanta". སྟོང་ཟིལ is taken as brass, and མཐིང་ཤུན as azurite flakes.  "Gives rise to that pride" refers to the divine pride of identifying with the deity. "What the omen has indicated" translates ལྟས་བསྟན་པ literally; it presumably means the sign received in the preliminary practice. In the hymn, སྟོབས་པོ་ཆེ is an epithet of Mañjuśrī ("of great strength"), not the deity Mahābala.  "Without slackening" renders ལྷུགས་མེད་པར, understood as qualifying the recitation; it could instead name a third kind of place. "At the doorway" renders ལམ་གོ, read as ལམ་སྒོ; the meaning is uncertain. "Holds sway over the earth" renders ས་གནོན, literally "presses the earth." "One who brings death" renders འཆི་བྱེད, which may refer to Yama (Antaka) or to a murderous enemy.  "Gla ba wood": གླ་བ ordinarily means the musk deer, which does not fit kindling sticks; the word is kept as written and the intended plant is uncertain. The buddha's name མེ་ཏོག་ཆེར་རྒྱས་རྒྱལ་བའི་དབང་པོ could not be identified with confidence and is translated literally as "Fully Blossomed Flower, Lord of Victors." The speaker addressing Śāntimati in the first person is presumably Vajrapāṇi. "Great Vajra Free of Extremes" here refers to that deity's mantra used for the oblation.  The buddha's name is spelled here མེ་ཏོག་ཆར་རྒྱས, read as ཆེར་རྒྱས as in the previous piece, and is kept as "Fully Blossomed Flower, Lord of Victors." "Colors not mixed with glue" reads སྦྱིན as སྤྱིན (glue); "inferior" reads ངན་དོན as ངན་ངོན. "Skya snar" is a flower that could not be identified with confidence and is left untranslated.  "The moon seized by the wolf" refers to a lunar eclipse. Several names could not be identified with confidence and are translated literally: the world system "Endowed with Flowers" (perhaps Kusumavatī), the bodhisattvas "Golden Flower Heap" and "Sandalwood Heap," and the śrāvakas "Steadfast Gift," "Vast Gift," "Essence Gift" and "Community Gift." "Phegdo" (ཕེག་རྡོ), apparently a musical instrument, and "śuti" (ཤུ་ཏི), apparently a substance or wood for the fire offering, could not be identified. "Gla ba wood" is kept as in the previous piece.  "Until the corpse's mouth makes a sound" renders the obscure རོ་ཁ་དང་ཞེས་བྱེད; the exact sense is uncertain. "Flowers that other mantrins cannot overcome" may refer to the aparājitā ("invincible") flower. "The binding of the person" (སྐྱེས་བུ་བཅིངས་པ) before crossing water is unclear in its exact sense. "Colors not mixed with glue" reads སྦྱིན as སྤྱིན (glue), as in the earlier painting rite. "Through him all the buddhas have brought about deliverance" renders the terse འདིས་སངས་རྒྱས་ཐམས་ཅད་བསྒྲལ་ཏོ.  The phrase rendered "if one scatters ... without asking" reads gtor nam blangs par, taken as gtor na ma blangs par, matching the parallel "without asking" earlier in the passage. "Human fat" renders mi shas, read as a ritual substance of human origin; the exact substance is uncertain. "Hammered wheel" renders ur mo'i 'khor lo; the sense of ur mo here is uncertain. "Again and again" renders la gor la gor du, whose meaning is uncertain. The tree name pata is given as spelled.  "And even beyond that" renders the obscure tshe bskal pa mchog pas kyang mchog thub pa. "Intermediate eon" takes che bar gyi bskal pa as bar gyi bskal pa; the reading is uncertain. "Again and again" renders la gor la gor du, whose exact sense is uncertain. In the sentence about the vidyādhara king's procession, the Tibetan does not mark clearly who pays homage to whom; it is taken as the many vidyādhara kings honoring him. Banda wood and torch wood (sgron shing) are uncertain identifications; nima pa is taken as nimba.  The line about the remaining petals reads 'dren mar, taken here as "to be drawn"; the intended color or sense is uncertain. The line on Candratilaka reads thob ste, which makes no sense here; it is taken as tho ba, "hammer," pairing with Trailokyavijaya's vajra. The door guardians thub dka' and mngon du gyur pa are rendered as Durdharṣa and Abhimukha, and the protectress 'jigs pa 'jigs byed as Bhayabhairavā; these Sanskrit forms are probable rather than certain.  "Held just short of touching" renders gang tsam gyis ma reg tsam, literally "not touching by about a measure"; the exact distance is not specified.  In the verse, gnon po ("oppressors"), grib gnon ("shadow-oppressors") and brjed byed ("causers of forgetting") are classes of harmful spirits rendered by meaning; brjed byed probably corresponds to apasmāra. Several mudrā names cannot be identified with confidence and are rendered by meaning: kun du brjod pa'i me ("all-proclaiming fire"), rdo rje kha ("Vajra Mouth"), rdo rje sen mo can ("Vajra Clawed One"), rdo rje rigs mdzes ma ("Vajra Beauty of the Family"), rdo rje lus dmar ("Vajra Red Body"), rdo rje 'jigs 'jigs lta ("Vajra Fearsome Gaze"), and dbu rgyan rtse bran ("Vajra Diadem, the playful servant"), where rtse bran is especially uncertain. Vajra Jhigara is given as the Tibetan spells it.  "Vajra shaft" renders ཡུ་བ (handle, shaft) literally. In the sequence of body-part mudrās, "vajra sign" renders མཚན, which here most likely denotes the private parts rather than a major mark, and "vajra portico" renders རྟ་བབས, whose sense in this series is unclear. "The Victor Śāntimati" takes ཞི་བའི་བློ་གྲོས་ཀྱི་རྒྱལ་བ as Śāntimati with the epithet Jina; it could also be read as "the victory mudrā of Śāntimati". "The Youth Born from the Crown" (གཞོན་ནུ་གཙུག་ནས་བྱུང་བ) is translated literally, as the figure could not be identified with confidence. "Doorkeepers" renders ཕྱག་བརྙན (Skt. pratihāra).  Several names of mudrās and spirit classes are uncertain. "Vajra subduer" (གནོན་པོ) and "vajra shadow-subduer" (གྲིབ་གནོན) are rendered literally; "apasmāra" takes བརྗེད་བྱེད as the standard rendering of that class of seizing spirits. Śumbha for གནོད་མཛེས, Susiddhikara for ལེགས་པར་གྲུབ་བྱེད, Āṭavaka for འབྲོག་གནས and Pāñcika for ལྔས་རྩེན are identifications by standard equivalence. "The Eight-Armed One" (ལག་པ་བརྒྱད་པ) is rendered literally. The name རྨུགས་བྱེད་འཛིན་ཆུ་དབང is unclear; it is given as "Jambhala Varuṇa", but it might name two figures or a holder of Jambhaka. The line marking the ninth section (bam po) of the text is kept as it stands. Pāñcika's speech continues into the next piece.  "The Averter of Austerities" renders དཀའ་ཐུབ་ཟློག literally; the deity could not be identified with confidence. Raudrī, Brāhmī, Aindrī, Vārāhī, Cāmuṇḍī and Kālarātrī are identified as the mother goddesses named here by their husbands or by standard Tibetan renderings. "The Lord of Death" renders འཆི་བདག, kept distinct from Yama (གཤིན་རྗེ). "The garuḍa Vinata" for རྣམ་པར་འདུད is tentative. "The eye" (སྤྱན) may refer to the goddess Locanā. "Grahas of sorcery" renders བྱད་ཀྱི་གདོན. The attribution "so the Victors have taught" closes the mudrā of Tumburu as the text has it.  Most names in the bodhisattva series are translated literally (Born from the Feet of the Tathāgata, the Wearer of Variegated Robes, Overcoming Infinite Space, Delightful to Behold, Ever Abiding in Diligence and the others), since their Sanskrit forms could not be fixed with confidence. Gaganāmala, Cāritramati, Sthiramati, Ratnākara and Ratnamudrāhasta are given by standard equivalence. Gaganāmala is named twice, for two different mudrās, as the text has it. བྱམས་པ is taken as "loving-kindness" in a series of qualities, though it could name Maitreya. ཀོསྐོ, a part of the Buddha's body in this series, could not be identified and is kept as "kosko".  "The Unfailing Vajra" renders རྡོ་རྗེ་དོན་ཡོད་པ (Skt. perhaps Amoghavajra), the name of the maṇḍala. "The vajra that summons and paralyzes" renders the unclear སྟང་སྟབས་འགུགས་པ. "The vajra of both" renders གཉིས་ཀའི་རྡོ་རྗེ literally.  The passage describes the setting out (nyāsa) of seed syllables on the parts of the body. The phrase rendered "with the double mark" is literally "with two increases" and most likely refers to the visarga, written as two dots; this identification is uncertain. "Powerful" (literally "endowed with strength") qualifying ha at the nose may likewise denote a written mark. The term rendered "breasts" is unusual in form and may refer to the breasts or the area around them. The mantras do not always match the syllables named in the verses; they are given as the text spells them.  The assembly addresses Vajrapāṇi as "Blessed One," as the Tibetan has it. The phrase rendered "great in might" qualifying the hosts of Māra is the same expression elsewhere used as the name Mahābala; here it reads as an epithet rather than a name.  "Shadow-seizing spirits" renders a Tibetan term literally meaning "shadow-pressers," apparently a class of graha that seizes through the shadow (cf. Skt. chāyāgraha); the Sanskrit identification is not certain. "Apasmāra" renders the Tibetan "those who cause forgetting," the usual equivalent for spirits causing seizures and loss of memory.  "Kṛtyās" renders the Tibetan term for malevolent sorcery and the spirits that carry it out. The Tibetan names of the tathāgatas are given in their standard Sanskrit forms: Ratnaketu, Saṃkusumitarājendra, Akṣobhya, Vipaśyin, Śikhin, Viśvabhū and Krakucchanda. "Praising" in the Ratnaketu mudrā follows the Tibetan as written; its sense in this gesture is unclear.  "Vaśavartin gods" renders the abode of those who exercise power over others' emanations (Paranirmitavaśavartin heaven). The statement that the mudrās "cut off the destinies" of gods, nāgas and the rest follows the Tibetan literally; it may mean that they sever rebirth in those states or that they hold sway over those beings.  Several lines of the stance list and the instructions are terse and uncertain: "ears drawn up and lip pressed down" renders a line that reads literally "rising from the ears, pressing the lip"; "restoring again" those of hostile mind renders ཕྱིར་གསོ་བ, which may instead mean "retaliating against"; "with supreme grace befitting the secret mantra" renders the obscure འཇོ་མཆོག; and "Brahmā's pillar" renders ཚངས་པའི་སྡོང་བུ, apparently the summit of the world reaching to Brahmā's realm. "The gods, the asuras and the guhyakas" takes གསང་བ as the class of guhyakas.  The passage begins mid-sentence, completing a phrase from the previous piece. The line "Lifting the left foot" occurs twice in succession in the Tibetan, and is kept as written. The line "with the right he should pierce" renders a line one syllable short in the Tibetan (གཡས་པས་ལ་གཟུག་པར་བྱ). "Set them in the vajra place" is uncertain: the object may be the mudrā rather than the spirits.  Several lines are terse and their sense uncertain. "Brought to a hundred straits" renders བརྒྱ་ལ་གྱུར, probably meaning "in dire distress." "The posture of Mahendra" takes དབང་པོ་ཆེན་པོ as the name of a stance. In "the vajra bearer, the wheel bearer," these may refer to Indra and Viṣṇu or to deities of the vajra and wheel families; the sense of the whole stanza ("if ... pays no heed to his own body") is uncertain. "The vajra place is defined from the waist" renders རྐེད་ནས་གཅོད literally.  The passage is terse throughout, and several lines are uncertain in sense, in particular the proclamation "If it is asked, 'Protect even a little!' ...", and the lines on forming the sword and sheath with the fingers. "The family of overpowering the adversary" renders ཕ་རོལ་གནོན་པའི་རིགས literally.  "The mare's mouth" (རྒོད་མའི་ཁ) refers to the mare-headed submarine fire of Indian myth (vaḍavāmukha), which drinks up the ocean. "Restore" renders གསོ་བ, which can also mean "heal" or "nourish." "The gods' triple banner" (ལྷ་ཡི་བ་དན་གསུམ) appears to name a hand gesture (perhaps tripatāka); "hip" for ཀོ་སྐོར is uncertain. "The one who arose from the crown" (གཙུག་ནས་བྱུང་བ) is unidentified and rendered literally.  The phrase rendered "resting the left hand at the hip" (ko skor brten) is uncertain; ko skor is not a common term, and the sense of placing the left hand at the hip or in a circle is inferred from the context of the dance gestures. "The renowned ones" (grags rnams) may possibly be a slip for the planets (gza'), who are named alongside the obstructors elsewhere in the passage, but it is kept as written. The lines "This is the vajra sound that resounds everywhere" and "This is the manifold vajra sound" appear to be labels naming the preceding mudrā sequences.  The term rendered "cupped hollow" (mtshog ma) for the joining of the palms is uncommon; the rendering is inferred from context. "The mouth of the mare" refers to the mare-headed submarine fire (Vaḍavāmukha) said to drink up the ocean's waters. The Mahendra stance and stride (dbang chen) are named postures of the ritual dance. "Then should make him flourish" renders rgyas par bya literally; it may refer to a rite of increase performed for the subdued obstructor.  "Disturbances" renders bar bur, an uncommon word; in the context of purifying the ground it may refer to uneven or obstructing matter, but the pacification later in the passage suggests obstructions in general. "Fire! Fire!" takes me me as the Tibetan word for fire uttered as a cry; it could conceivably be a Sanskrit syllable string. "The five heroic postures" renders dpa' bo lnga po, literally "the five heroes"; the exact reference is unclear.  "Should set the guhyakas to their tasks on every side" renders gsang ba kun du gtad par bya; it could also be read as entrusting something secret, and the sense is uncertain. The line about Vāsuki (nor rgyas bu sprul gsang bsrel yin) is obscure; it is rendered as Vāsuki, an emanated son, kept at the secret place, but the meaning is doubtful. The passage includes a fascicle marker, "the eleventh fascicle," which is part of the text.  The nine modes listed are the nine sentiments (rasas) of Indian dance and drama, here given as the erotic, heroic, compassionate, comic, wrathful, terrifying, dreadful, wondrous and peaceful. "Hell beings" renders sems dmyal, evidently short for the beings of the hells. In "let fall" the text reads da bab, taken as dbab, "to cause to fall."  "Behind the back" reads རྒྱབ་རོལ for the written རྒྱལ་རོལ, which makes no sense here. "The swan's prowess" in the verse reads ངང་པ for the written དང་པ, following the closing label of the same mudrā. The lines describing the buddhas and bodhisattvas (གཞན་དབང་ནི་ཐོབ་འགྱུར་ར་རྣམས, བློང་ཞེས་གྱུར་ནས) are obscure: གཞན་དབང is taken here in its ordinary sense, "under another's power" (that is, overpowered by Vajrapāṇi), not as the doctrinal "dependent nature", and བློང is taken as "dazed". ལུང here is rendered "command" in the narrative sense, not "scripture".  "Buddha Ornament Melody" translates the name of the tathāgata literally, since its Sanskrit form cannot be identified with confidence. In the prose, the written དངངས is read as ངས, "I", which the sentence requires. In the offering-diadem verse the line དེ་ཡི་ཚེ་ནས་ལ་བཞག is a syllable short and is read as ས་ལ་བཞག, "place them on the ground".  The written ནད་པར is read as ནོད་པར, "receive", which the parallel lines require. "Essence of the Ornament of Conduct" translates the name of the tathāgata literally, since its Sanskrit form cannot be identified with confidence. The line on "the gaping mouth of the mare" is obscure; it may refer to beings dwelling in, or likened to, the mare's mouth, the submarine fire.  The world system and the tathāgata are both named Stainless Light Rays; the name is translated literally, since its Sanskrit form cannot be identified with confidence. Source of All is likewise a literal rendering of the dhāraṇī's name.  The line གྲུ་བཞི་ལྷམ་པ་ཁ་ཁྱེར་བཅས is obscure; "with its borders and portals" is a tentative rendering of the maṇḍala's architectural features. "Invincible Vajra Flame", "Who Abandons All Misdeeds" and "Hard to Tame" translate the goddesses' names literally, since their Sanskrit forms cannot be identified with confidence. "The maṇḍala of Mahendra" and "water", "fire" and "wind" refer to the elemental maṇḍalas on which the emblems are placed.  "The Blessed Lady Who Accomplishes the Infinite Gateways" renders a Tibetan name that matches the title of the Anantamukha dhāraṇī; "the Blessed Lady Who Impels" is translated from the Tibetan meaning, as its Sanskrit form cannot be identified with confidence. The phrase rendered "as though in a single day" could also be read as "day after day."  "Birch bark" reads the Tibetan as written, "grog" (and "grogs") as a slip for "gro ga," birch bark, the usual surface for amulet writing; as written it would mean "ant" or "friend," which do not fit. In "ten buddha fields," the instrumental particle after "buddha" is read as a genitive. A stray syllable "naṃ" after "that mantra practitioner" has no sense in context and is left unrendered. The reply beginning "Mañjuśrī, it is just as you have said" carries no named speaker in the Tibetan; from context it is Vajrapāṇi.  The reply beginning "Mañjuśrī, excellent, excellent!" carries no named speaker in the Tibetan; from context it is Vajrapāṇi.  "At the price asked" takes the Tibetan "kha thabs bzhin du" as buying the cloth without haggling, the usual rule for ritual materials; the phrase is uncertain. "Protected with the Cakravartin" most likely refers to a protective mantra or deity of that name used in the rite. The Tibetan "rdo rje mi pham pa'i me" before "white lotus" is read as a slip for the feminine name "rdo rje mi pham pa ma," Vajrājitā, which fits the following feminine attributes; this identification is tentative.  In the first invisibility rite the Tibetan as written says the body "becomes visible" (lus ni snang bar 'gyur); since the rite is explicitly for invisibility, it is read as "invisible," the negative evidently lost. "Dang pa" is read as a slip for "ngang pa," haṃsa, which fits the mount that makes one "a second Brahmā." "Zho" in the list of boons is read as "zhon," a mount; as written it means curds. "Nad par" is read as "nang par," in the morning. "Ligbu mik" (lig bu mig) is an ingredient that could not be identified with confidence and is given in phonetics. The Sanskrit phrase in the second-to-last rite means "it grants kingship."  The Sanskrit phrase rājyaṃ pratilabhyate, left untranslated in the Tibetan, means "one obtains kingship." "When the moon is seized by the wolf" (written སྦྱང་ཀི, read as སྤྱང་ཀི) evidently denotes a lunar eclipse. The plant names spoma (སྤོ་མ), kalapiṅka, paṭa and brama (བྲ་མ) could not be identified with confidence and are given as they stand. "The Husband of Umā" renders དཀའ་ཐུབ་ཟློག་གི་ཁྱོ, literally "the husband of the one turned back from austerities," a Tibetan rendering of Umā's name; it refers to Śiva. ཁམ་བྱེ་བ is read as ཁ་མ་བྱེ་བ, "unopened," as elsewhere in the passage. The exact sense of ལྕགས་ཨུར་མོ (rendered "iron") and of གློར ("at one's side") is uncertain.  ཚེར་འཐབ་པ ("they will fight") is unclear in its first element; the sense of strife is evident. མྱ་ངན་འཚང is taken as a variant name of the aśoka tree. The plant names ligadur (ལི་ག་དུར) and geushar (སྒེའུ་གཤེར) could not be identified with confidence and are given phonetically. ཁམ་བྱེ་བ is read as ཁ་མ་བྱེ་བ, "unopened." "All the Śrīs" renders དཔལ་ཇི་སྙེད་ཡོད་པ, taken as forms of the goddess of fortune parallel to the yakṣiṇīs, mātṛkās and Sarasvatīs in the surrounding lines. The Sanskrit line after the jātī-leaf rite concerns bringing a king under control by a fire offering of a hundred thousand oblations; the one after the bilva rite appears to concern bestowal of kingship.  The romanized Sanskrit lines are left untranslated in the Tibetan; they concern, respectively, the bestowal of kingship by the goddess, bringing a king under control by a fire offering of eight thousand oblations, and bringing a great king and his chief queen under control. "Milky-sapped wood" renders ཤིང་འོ་མ་ཅན (Skt. kṣīravṛkṣa); madaka (ཤིང་མ་ད་ཀ) is given as transcribed.

The following is a note about this translation.

This is an AI-generated translation from the AI Stupa project. It was produced by a careful, doctrine-aware process aiming at a high degree of accuracy, but it has not been reviewed by a human translator and may contain errors. It is offered as a provisional translation for those texts of the Tibetan Buddhist canon that have not yet received an authoritative human translation. Where a published translation exists — above all from 84000: Translating the Words of the Buddha (84000.co) — that translation is definitive and should be preferred; please consult it instead. These translations are released into the public domain (CC0) in the spirit of spreading the Dharma to all corners of the earth.

Just as the Bodhi tree now standing at Bodh Gayā neither is nor is not the same tree beneath which the Buddha attained awakening, so too this stupa is intended as the next generation of stupa — dependently arising according to the conditions of its environment, and ceaselessly proclaiming the Dharma for the benefit of all sentient beings.

May whoever encounters these words derive great benefit.
